TRADING IN EGGS.

Fowl Keepers and the Need for a Quick Sale of the Perishables.

FEEDING YOUNG TURKEYS.

When an article deteriorates so rapidly as an egg, it is of the utmost importance that the greatest promptness be exercised in all methods of trading, and that it shall pass through as few hands as possible before it reaches its final destination. When the producer is in the en- viable position of being able to get into direct communication with the consumer he possesses Opportunities which should never be neglected. When go placed his advantages are numerous. In the first case, considerably better prices are obtained, since the intermediate charge is non-existent, and it is in the consumer's own hands to make the best bargain possible. All benefits accruing therefrom go directly into his own pocket. Furthermore, eggs can pass be- tween buyer and seller much more rapidly without the intervention of a middleman, and Thus the eggs may be in their first bloom of freshness when they reach the consumer. In this manner a reputation for quality is obtained, which is one of the finest recommendations That can be possessed by the producer.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
POULTRY IN CONFINEMENT.

Perhaps the advantage of confinement which will appeal to the poultry-keeper more strongly than anything else is the fact that a higher egg yield may confidently be expected from hens under these conditions than when part of their energy goes in another direction, as it does when they wander at liberty. This is probably the chief reason why more eggs are usually produced by fowls in confinement. Poultry in runs are also tamer, owing to their being kept under a better state of domestication, which invariably tends to increased productiveness. Furthermore, when several breeds are to be kept pure for the sake of sittings of eggs— which is one of the most lucrative branches of poultry-keeping--no difficulty is experienced in keeping the breeds apart, so that purity of race can be guaranteed, which is not always pos- sible when they are at liberty. It is also easier to observe the laying of the pullets, and other points, with a view to their selection for future breeding stock, and these characteristics are easier to discern when ttie owner gets into Close touch with his birds.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE FEEDING OF YOUNG TURKEYS.

The feeding of growing turkeys, says Mr. E. T. Brown in "Farm and Home," is extremely important, and the food should contain the necessary elements for the development of the frame. Fattening foods should be avoided, for the desire is to build up a large and strong frame. Fattening foods are not required till the middle of November, when the last stage in the life of a turkey commences. The birds must not be stinted of food, for this is kise economy. At the same time, waste must be avoided, and the tuzlieys sliotuld be encouraged to-scratch about, and obtain-for theniselves a portion of their oWn living. wet-summer two meals daily are sufficient. If the weather is dry, three meals must be provided. The first meal of the day should consist of meals, and a suitable ration is the following: Two parts barley meal, two parts middlings, one part ground oats, half a part bean or pea meal, and half a part lean meat. An occasional meal of boiled rice is recommended, not only on account of its excellent feeding properties, but because it acts as a preventive against diarrhcea. In the afternoon, about 4.30 at the present time, and a little earlier when the days begin to draw in, some grain should be pro- vided, preferably wheat, oats, and buckwheat. A little hemp seed assists greatly when the new 'leathers are forming. If a third meal is given, it should take place at midday, and mash—the same mixture as that used for breakfast—is suitable.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
TRE PROFITABLE GOOSE.

When it is remembered that an adult goose produces on an average about licwt. of manure in the course of a twelvemonth,' it is not surprising that the land derives great bene- fit. It is sometimes thought that other kinds of stock will not follow geese in a meadow, but this has never been my experience, unless the land is stocked too heavily, when it would apply to any kind of poultry. A few weeks

ago we came across a small-holder whose hold- ing was about ii acres, and who, for several years past, has more than made the rent of his entire holding with the gosnngs he rears. He is fortunately situated for this kind of work, his place bordering on a common. His birds sleep in sheds on his holding, but first thing in the morning they are driven forth on to the common, where they remain contentedly all day long, returning as night approaches.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
UTILITY POULTRY CLUB.

The Utility Poultry Club, who applied for grants from the Development Fund, have now heard from the Commissioners. The applica- tion to enable another twelve months' competi- tion to be held at the Harper Adams College has been granted, and a sum of £225 has been placed at the disposal of the club, in accordance with the scheme and estimates lodged with the application. The Commissioners have not yet definitely decided whether they can make a grant towards the suggested competition, to be managed by Mr. J. N. Leigh at Seddles- combe, near Battle, for the purpose of testing two methods of poultry-keeping—ordinary and semi-intensive—but it is expected that a favourable reply will be received. As regards the proposal for a progeny test, the Commis- sioners are, at present, unable to come to a decision, as they consider it is essential that scientific authorities should first be consulted. The Committee of the Club held a meeting on Wednesday, when the details of the two com- petitions were settled, and it is Eloped to issue them to the public shortly. Arrangements are being made for accommo- dating fifty pens of six birds each, in the exist- ing plant at the Harper Adams College, to which certain improvements are to be made. This plant was acquired by means of a grant of £5OO out of the Development Fund last year, and is now being used for the present competition. On this account the new compe- tition will not begin until the end of October. The competition at Seddlescombe will, it is anticipated, be run in two sections, with thirty-two pens of four birds in each, and the competitors will be required to enter a pen in both sections, in order that the two systems may be tested. The competition should prove extremely interesting, and ought to receive ade- tmate support from the poultry-keepers.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
FARM AND GARDEN.

CULTIVATING SECOND HAY CROP..

Taking a second crop of hay is; of .course, more exhausting to the land than takifig One and grazing the afterinft*; but the plan is quite justified where hay is badly wanted, owing to the first crop having been light, or where hay is in good demand in a town close at hand. Next year's crop need not suffer if you manure the meadows for it, but you should certainly not omit that. As for manuring for the second cut, nitrate of soda will be the right thing, given at the rate of one cwt. per acre as soon as possible. After- math hay is never as good in quantity nor quality as the first crop, unless it was very badly harvested. Owing to its rather washy nature it needs to be well made before being carted.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
COWS HOLDING UP THE MILK.

Cows are often "difficult " in regard to giv- ing down their milk perfectly in the height of the milking season. They tall into the nabit in the flush of feed from tenderness of the udder, especially if the spwes between milk- ings are unequal, so that at one of them the bag becomes very much overstrained and pain- ful. The bag thus becomes inflamed, swollen, and hard, and withholding the milk or any part of it tends to increase the difficulty. From an unequal division of time between milking, some cows habitually bold up their milk at night, and give it down in the morn- ing. The habit once being formed, there is no sure cure for it. The same treatment will not work alike on different cows. As a rule, cows give down best in a few minutes after they first come into the stable or yard, without hav- ing any food before them or anything to attract attention or disturb them, but some will give their milk more freely if eating something that they relish well, so that their attention is more taken up with the food than with the milking. Regular, evenly-timed, quick, quiet, and com- fortable milking is essential, and the best means both for preventing and for breaking up the bad habit in. cows of holding up their milk.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
FERTILITY OF THE FARM.

" That permanent success in agriculture de- pends upon the fertility of the whole farm, is one of the principles of soil fertility which is in danger of being forgotten," declares Professor A. 11. Whitson, of the College of Agriculture of the University of Wisconsin. "Many farmers raise heavy crops on a small acreage by apply- ing special treatment and using on that one field a large part of the manure produced on the whole farm, but leaving the larger fields of the farm in a low state of fertility.

"This is wrong on both sides. The soil of the field which is kept in an abnormally high state of fertility is liable to loss of fertility by leaching, while the fields which are in a very low state of fertility are being unprofitably farmed. Loss of fertility occurs on the one hand and loss of labour on the other. What is needed is a proper balance, so that the fer- tility of the whole farm is raised to as nigh a point as practicable. Do not leave the back fields uncared for. If some fields are far away from the barn or are located on hills, so that it is impracticable to haul manure to them, these fields should be seeded to clover once in the rotation, and either the whole crop or at least the second crop turned under, together with a good application of some phosphate fertiliser."

===== 0002601_19130830_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
PEDIGREE Oft CROSS-BRED PIGS.

The average breeder of pigs, says a writer in " Farm and Home," is not a producer of breed- ing stock but of meat for sale as pork or bacon. His object is to obtain as great a weight of car- case, the meat being of good quality and con- taining a fair proportion of lean to fat, which should never be too abundant, in as short a space of time as possible. He uses lengthy sows because they produce lengthy pigs, and this is all-Important. If we increase the weight of the best mltat we iltrease tht returns from each pig slaughtered. N,Vhether it istetter to breed from a pedigreed sow or a cross-bred sow is a point about which much more might be said than is possible in the space at our disposal. First, it is well to note that a pedigree may mean nothing at an. If, for instance, a sow is small, short,rthick in the neck, prick-eared, shorit-faced, or it she is lanky, flat-sided, with weak loins and hams, with coarse hair; or if she possesses some of these faults, the fact that she is pedigree bred is of no value whatever.

A sow may win a first prize at the Royal, and produce one or two gilts which are very imperfect, and whicfr in consequence are of no value for stock. When pedigree is allied to size, form, quality and constitution, its value is great, and every means possible siTould be adopted to retain it, and to perpetuate the qualities which are represented. The mistake is in breeding from second-class pigs, because they possess a pedigree. On the oiler hand, there are many very handsome sows which are cross-bred, a cross-bred sow being one which is the result of the union of a sire and dam of dis- tinct Pure-bred varieties. Such a sow is of much greater value than a weedy pig with a pedigree.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE GARDEN. A FEW REMINDERS.

Prick out seedling pansies, violas, snap, wall- flowers, itc., in a nursery bed and keep moist. Take cuttings of lobelia, or small pieces of growths with roots attached, and plant in boxes, for the provision of stock from which to propagate in the spring. The boxes must be kept under glass. Take cuttings of geraniums and insert in pots of sandy soil. Let the cuttings remain for a day or so before potting. Divide pleonies as soon as the foliage is becom- ing dry. The succulent roots will then have a longer period to become settled. Moisten the soil if dry. 'Place bulbs of early narcissi in pots in a mix- ture of loam, and, and leaf-soil. Cover the pots with ashes Until rooted. Gather shallots as soon as the tops have died down, and store in a dry, cool place. Dig potatoes as soon as the haulm is fully ripened. Take cuttings of violas, cutting below a leaf- joint. Remove the lower leaves and insert in soil to which a liberal quantity of sand has been added. There are often young growths with roots attached which root readily. Violas and pansies can only be kept true to names by cuttings.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
SUPERSEDING BEDDING PLANTS.

This is a question which is being asked by gardeners who do their own work. Is It worth the expense and trouble of growing plants whfch last only, about three months? In most cases the expense comes every year, because the majority of amateurs have not the accom- modation for keeping and raising plants each year. The same amount of money laid out on perennials will, it is contended, give infinitely better results and produce flowers more useful for cutting. Such plants would be permanent and by root division the garden can be further beautified. There are many who will not fore- go the usual bedding plants, but the growers of hardy flowers have recognised the fact that they are of More value for the single-handed man than the summer bedders.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
HYACINTHS IN GLASSES.

All too frequently these are not dealt with early enough, and for this reason the period of flowering is delayed. During the present month is an excellent time to, place the first hyacinth bulb in glasses. Another batch can be dealt with later on, so as to have a fresh lot of plants, as the first batch goes out of flower. A Record Increase in the Importation of Margarine. AMERICAN PRODUCE LESS.

An interesting account of the amount of agri- cultural imports into this country during 1912 is given in tile statistics fur last year just issued by the Board of Agriculture. The imports of dead meat increased by 215 per cent., which seems to indicate that tile people of Britain are becoming larger cbrisumers of frozen meat. This is probably due to the increased cost of home-fed meat. 'the imports of live cattle, sheep and pigs have, however, decreased by 30 per cent. Dairy produce imported shows an increase of 130 per cent. The year 1912, says the report, was, in sonic respe.its, abnormal as regards supplies of meat. IThe number of cattle imported alive has been declining year 1;y year since 1905, when 565,000 were landed. In 1911 the number had fallen to 200,000, but in 1912 it fell to less than one-fourth of that, the total received being only 49,000. Imports of live sheep, which practically ceased in 1910, but recovered slightly in 1911, fell again to 15,060 in 1912. In 1895 the lc/Tat imported was over 1,000,000, and for Ifie 'four succeeding years it was about two-thirds of that number. The decadence of the trade in animals," on the hoof" has been balanced by the increased imports of animals "in the carcase." The quan- tity of fresh beef imported in 1912 exceeded, for the first time, 8,000,000 cwts., having doubled in ten years. The imports of fresh mutton amounted to over 5,000,000 cwts., beingsome. what less than in 1910 and 1911, but over 1,000,000 cwts. more Wan they were ten years ago. Taking all kinds of meat together, the aggregate oversea receipts were in 1912, as in 1911, about 21,000,000 cwts., but the value was nearly £2,500,000 more, tile total reaning £46,521,000.

NO SUBSTANTIAL INCREASE.

On Inc whole, however, if all kinds of meat, pigmeat, in- eludingi;e.beef, veal, h 111 ipuot ft* en(-; alive,tt aand a taliell into account, it is curious that the aggregate im- ports have not substantially increased in recent years, or at any rate have not kept pace with the growth of population. It cannot be said that this is due to increased home supplies. There was an increase in the total supply of nearly if, million cwts. on the average of the later six ycars,of which about 800,000 was in home supplies and about 400,000 in imported supplies. This increase, however, has not kept pace with the growth of population. The aver- age supply per head during the earlier six years was 1331 b., while during the later six years it was 12911 b. The reduction in oversea supplies alone amounts to lilb. per head of population. Thf,re appears to have been no indication of a falling oh f in the relative position of home sup- plies, about 59 per cent. of the total meat sup- ply being produced in the United Kingdom on the average of the whole period. The significant, and possibly from the eon- sumer's point of view some;vhat ominous, fact is that oversea supplies show insufficient ex- pansion; or to speak more precisely, that the contraction of the supplies from Nort7America, on which reliance has for so long been placed, has not been balanced by the increased sup- plies from the Southern hemisphere to the ex- tent necessary to meet the increased demand. During the past six years the quantity of dead meat received from the United States has de- clined from 6/ million cwts. in 1907 to less than three million cwts. in 1912, while those from Canada have fallen from nearly one mil- lion cwts. to less than half a million cwts. Sup- plies from New Zealand have shown no expan- sion, and practically the whole of the increased supplies have come from Argentina and Ausftalia.

PLACING WINDOW PLANTS IN RAIN.

One frequently sees ferns, aspidistras and palms, also other kinds of plants, put outside during rain showers so that the leaves may be washed clean. It is a very good plan to treat the plants in this way when they are placed in a shaded position. The mistake generally made is to • cxpose the plants to sudden bursts of strong sunshine, as well as to the rain, with the result that many leaves which have never before been exposed to the sun's rays get badly scalded. There would be no risk if the plants were takon indoors again before the sun shone or if they, were put in a position not exposed to the sunshine:

===== 0002601_19130830_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PANTHER LILY.

In liliurn pardannum, the panther lily, we possess a particularly useful tall-growing lily, which flowers from the middle of July until the Middle.ot August. A native of North America, the usual heikTht is from sft. to 71t. Both leaves and flowers are arranged in whorls on the tall stems, giving the plants a distinct and stately character. Each stern terminates in a loose inflorescence of tyelve to twenty blooms, rich orange in colour and freely spotted. It is what may be termed a good,natured lily, for the bulbs appear to thrive equally well in peat or moderately light loam: L. pardalinum has large, rhizomatous bull* which increase rapidly. These should be planted 5 inches to 6 inches deep, as this is a stem-rooting species. One of the best positions for this lily is in beds of rhododendrons, where the stems will be screened from the hot sun.—" The Garden."

THO CHESHIRE SHOW. -fewer Entries but Larger

Attendance.

The annual show of the Cheshire Agricul- tural Society, held at Chester on Wednesday, was a great 'success, notwithstanding the fact that the elimination of the pigeon and poultry and butter-making competitions caused a de- crease in the number of entries—from 1.07 to 1,781. Ideal weather prevailed, and there was an absence of such restrictions as those which operated twelve months ago as to the move- ment of cattle. The total attendance was 11,031, as against 6,021 last year, 9,620 in 1911, and 8,341 in 1910. Apart from the live stock on view, the day's programme embraced scvcral features of interest. An innovation w;ts the military tournament held during the afternoon, and other attractions were demonstrations in beekeeping by the Bev. T. J. Evans, an •xhibi- Lion of nature study works by County Counen pupils, and selections by Foderes Motor Works Band.

The entries in ne L-nre horse class were dis- tinctly good. Many of the. arrington winnors were on view. One of the best of the young classes was that for yearling fillies, in which the Warrington decisions were rather upset. In the cattle classes the dairy section was naturally the most numerous and interesting, and some very line milking animals, possessing the best dairy qualities, were exhibited. The competition in the cheese classes, in which the. entries numbered over 120, was stronger than it has been for some years. The summer has been very favourable, and only two " running " exhibits were found by the judges. In the coloured section the quality was unusually good, and the judges called in a col- league before they came to a decision. Mr. Edwin Cookson, of Poulton, took the first prize and the Duke of Westminster's championship cup.

FARMERS AND TARIFF REFORM.

A ONE-SIDED SCHEME THAT HAS NO ATTRACTION FOR

AGRICULTURISTS.

This is a pamphlet written by Mr. Beriri:-.1 Gilbert, a practical farmer. It seems that while politicians have been interesting them- selves in Home Rule, Welsh Disestablishment, and Marconis, the farmers have been thinking hard on the subject of what is called here "the new scheme of Tariff Reform." Mr. Seth Ellis Dean, chairman of the Lincolnshire Farmers' Union, writes a preface, in which he re-affirms the resolution passed by the National Farmers' Union to the effect that they would be no party to a change of policy that excluded agriculture from its benefits.

We had almost forgotten Mr. Bonar Law's speech at Ashton-under-Lyne, and the situation which it created, but it is all recalled here with a refreshing degree of candour and indignation. The ground of discontent is that the farmers are to pay taxes on the things they buy—on mach- inery, manure and tools, under the head of "manufactured articles," .an4cthat they are to get no benefit of a tax on articles of food which they. sell. This one-sided scheme de- prives Tariff Reform of every attraction it had for farmers. They see "the rise of the black- smith's bill, the implement .dealer's bill, the cake bill," and they see the increase in the cost of clothing, with no compensating increase in the cost of wheat, dairy produce, or meat. The reply to the proposal given in the pamphlet is that "farmers are not fools." They do not mean to accept this unequal policy. They must "insist upon a complete all-round scheme of Tariff Reform, or that it be dropped alto- gether." The statement of the case by Mr. Gilbert is clear and forcible. It is a farmer's complaint voiced in farmer's language. Supported by quotations from leaders of the Unionist party and from prominent landowners and farmers, the demand for a definite pronouncement is advanced. There is no attempt to bring in party politics, but the plea for fair and impartial treatment for farmers is urged in a fair style and should appeal to every one interested in this important question.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
AUSTRALIA'S LARGE CITIES.

AN ATTEMPT TO DIVERT IMMIGRANTS FROM THE TOWNS TO THE COUNTRY.

A common criticism of Australian develop- ment is that its cities are far too large. For Instance, nearly one-third of the entire popula- tion of the six States is assembled in Sydney and Melbourne. In view of this, it is satisfac- tory to notice that the immigration policy now promoted by the Commonwealth and State Governments aims specially to attract rural settlers. Little or no encouragement is given to city workers of any kind. Mr. Hagelthorn, a member of the Government in Victoria, pointed out recently that the total number of assisted immigrants to land in Victoria in 1912 was 14,016. Of these, farmers'and land-seekers with their wives and families represented 1,557 and farm labourers, including wives . and families, 2,305. In addition to this, a substan- tial proportion of the remaining persons also proceeded to country districts. During the first six months of 1913, 385 land-seekers, inaluding wives( and families.. 985 V arm labourers, and 485 lads, entered Victoria. Altogether, about 7,000 people arrived during these six months, and of this number 3,480 made their homes in rural districts. The great attraction Victoria now offers to country workers is, of course, upon its irrigated areas. The demand for Irrigated farms has been considerably in excess of the Government's expectation, and large ad- ditional works to make more irrigable land available are now foreshadowed.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

A Little Grumble over the Insurance Act.

NO "REFRESHING FRUIT."

At the fortnightly meeting of Runcorn Board of Guardians, held on Tuesday afternoon at Dutton Workhouse, Mr. C. E. Linaker, jun., presided. The Master reported that ten pigs had been sold, and the amount realised was £67 O. Bd. Ile asked permission to purchase a new lot of pigs. A vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. T. Wardle, of Sutton, for gift of flowers to the Workhouse. It -was recommended that a re- volving shelter be purchased, at a cost of about £l5. for the treatment of phthisis cases at the workhouse.

It was re”orted that the house was now ade- quately staffed, with the exception of a charge nurse. Three separate advertisements had ap- peared and a salary of £3O a year offered, but this had not proved an attraction and there had not been one solitary reply. The committee recommended that a new advertisement be in- serted and the salary increased to £35. The number of inmates in the workhouse last week was 120. compared with 128 last year. There were 91 vagrants relieved, as compared with 58 last year. The minutes were confirmed, on the motion of the Chairman.

Does the Insurance Act Give Relief?

Mr. Linaker, sen., said that he had read a report where it was stated that the total sum granted to old-age pensioners throughout the country amounted to £8,000,000, and this gave relief to the poor rate in some cases varying from sevenpence to a shilling in the He would like to know whether they had anything to show that the Runcorn Union was receiving anything like that amount of relief from the Act. _ _ .

The Clerk said that they were, of course, not getting anything like such benefit. There might be a decrease of a penny in the in respect of the number:of paupers taken off the relieving officers' books, but their expenses in other directions had gone up, and he did not promise they would see any actual reduction in the poor rate in the future.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
Did Not Help.

Mr. Linaker, Sen.: So it is a fact that we don't actually get any of "that rare and refreshing fruit" we were promised. The Chairman said he read the report referred to hy Mr. Linaker, but he was of the opinion that the Act had made "a good show" in the towns, but it had not done much in the country.

Mr. Wood said it was admitted that the num- ber of persons in receipt of outdoor. relief had been diminished by nine per cent. The Chairman said that the rates had gone up. Mr. Wood said that this was due to extra charges on the maintenance of the roads and other parts of their administration. The Clerk said that there was certainly not the same amount of outdoor relief by a penny in the but the expenses of the Guardians were increasing. The new Children's Home was going to cost the Union some hundreds of pounds and there was a slight increase in the county call.

The Chairman: So the amount saved in one way is absorbed in another direction.

Striking Figures.

Mr. Wood said that the figures of indoor relief were very striking. The number of vagrants had increased from 58 to 91. This was extra- ordinary, especially when farmers were telling them that in the country at the present time they could not possibly get men. Did the BoarZ think it was attributable to the new arrangements made for relief? Mr. Pearson had told them that he had only given one vagrant's ticket in a month. and Mr. Cook had only had 14 in a month. Ilad they made the system too easy? The Chairman said that was a matter for dis- cussion.

Mr. Wright: They are mostly old people.

Delegates to Conference.

In accordance with notice given, Mr. Wood- ward moved that Messrs. C. E. Linaker, jun., the Vice-Chairman, and Thomas Wood attend as delegates to the North-Western Poor Law Con- ference at Preston, on the 3rd and 4th October. Mr. Woodward asked for authority to attend at his own expense as the Guardians' representative and this was granted. Messrs. Hayes, Harrison and Mclntyre were appointed to attend Pant- asaph Orphanage. Messrs. G. Pilling, J T. Bax- ter and Horton were elected delegates to the Asylum. This resolution was passed.

POINTS FOR TARIFF REFORMERS.

THE PROPHET.

As to the prospect of any return to Protection in any shape or form, I think it is inconceivable that the agricultural interest would allow manu- facturers to be protected while food imports went free.—Mr. Joseph Chamberlain, at the Eighty Club, April 28th, 1885.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SHIP CANAL.

I have never yet understood the idea of creating an artificial dearness; and the whole trend of industry, and the object of our modern commercial organisation is to be able to pro- duce more commodities at a lower rate. :You in Lanbashire spent an immense amount of money on a ship canal. What was the object? To bring foreign goods more cheaply into Lan- cashire, and to take your goods out. But, ac- cording to the Tariff Reformers, you have com- mitted a great sin. Instead of imposing a tariff, therefore, you Cave only to fill up the Ship Canal, and you will make the goods dearer, and you will thus achieve one of the great objects of Tariff Reform, and keep these goods out. These people never realise one of the most elementary economic laws, and that is, that as people do not give you something for nOthing, obviously if you get something from them they must take something from you. That being admitted, obviously that country which gets in exchange for the commodities it exports the largest amount of imports, will become the richest country. Those who realise this always remain Free Traders. It is those who never .grasp the very simple theory that goods pay for goods, who still cling to the obsolete idea that by the exclusion or making dearer of foreign goods you caii increase the wealth of this or any other countty.—Sir A. Mond, at lirmston.

HOW TO SUCCEED AS

A CLERK.

Two boys enter an altos at the same are, With apparently the same education and with the same opportunities. After the lapse of a certai• iaterval of time one is getting a handsome salary and the other knows that his highest earnings will never be more than two pounds a week. What is the reason for the difference ? The unsucces,iful man says it is either favouritism or luck. The em- ployer knows that it is neither. Whatever WC may understand by the word luck, no one seriously believes tliat, in the majority of cases, success is anything else but the reward of merit.

The first cause of failure is gett.!ng into a groove. The man becomes a machine; Tho writer of this article asked a lara." emnlover • . of labour how many of his clerks posse,,ed either origl/2iality or power of initiative and he replied, "Not one in fifty." The one was the man that "got on."

===== 0002601_19130830_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
E=..RGY AND LOYALTY.

The second cause of failure is lack of ener- gy and loyalty. The man who measures his work by the amount of pay he receives is likely to have hie merits estimated in terms 0.1 his present rather than his luture pay. I knew a boy who went at sixteen into a Lon- don merchant's office. He would stay to any hour to finish his work and never grumbled when asked, to help w.th a long dose of over- time at seasons of great pressure. At nine- teen he was sent to a foreign .branch with a salary of £3OO a .year and a prospect Of fur- ther advancement if he continued to serve Fiz employers with the same devotion. An- other, a scholarship boy from an elementary 4211001, gave such satisfaction in the same way, to the directors of a great railway corn.. pany that though he is still young, and ig- norant of the fate that is in store for him, he has been already marked out for promotion and by the time he is forty he will be on the way to a salary of over a thousand a year. Neither of these boys had influence or money. They had pluck, energy and cheerfulness. Succee.3 is the product of many factors— manners, clothes, speech, education, ability. A man may possess all but one of these and so reduce his value to nothing, for any number of high numbers multiplied by nethine, will give zero as the final result. Consirer the man who is' wasteful in small things such as sta- tionery, the one who is discourteous and un- civil, the one who is inattentive to instruc- tions so that they have to be repeated, the one who is untidy in his clothes and personal appearance, and the one who is unpunctual in coming or early going, and then say to what extent they must be capable and ener- getic to make up for these faults. There is another man in the office who possesses aLt your good qualities and those you lack by.* sides, and he will leave you far behind in the race for promotion. He has no noughts amongst the factors that his employers look for when they wish to bestow any office of re- sponsibility or trust upon one of their staff.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
MENTAL EQUIPMENT.

Leaving aside the moral qualities that make for fame and wealth let us consider the mental equipment of the clerk. First of all he should have a good memory. It is not our busintess to point out here how this is to Ix acquired but the ability to carry easily and with unfailing accuracy a mass of detail, either important. or trivial is of the highest importance. Then there is the question of English composWon. Not one c'erk in a hundred can write decent English. And what is worse than . the inability to write good English is the ignorance that one. is wanting in th.s partcular. If you have not the education necessary for the composition of decent prose you have not got the meaneof detecting the weakness or the error of what you put down on paper. How many times do we get different to instead of different from, and shoals of similar faults. A term or two at an evening school would soon put an end to these elementary errors of expres- sion. There is FO many cases too, a strange want of interest in the work in hand. I man will spend years in the office of a firm of shippers and never know where the places are located to which the vessels sail, never know anything of the source or the use of the goods tilat the firm handles. Commereial geography may not be required of the thirty- shillings-a-week clerk but a little profession- al interest in the trade of the employer may well be looked for from the manager or WO chairman of the d.rectors. Most clerks know more about the results of the last series of football matches than they do of the move- ments in the trad•e to which they owe their living. Clerks as a body waste their leisure on trivial and fr'yoloue matters. They read inane books and the least useful parts of the newspapers and they spend fixer time in search of amusement instead of in search of improvement.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
A BUSINESS MAN'S OPINION.

The writer has had experience, in one way 3r another, of hundreds of clerks and he has been sorrowfully if powerfully impressed liy their sad lack of interest in the things that appertain to their real welfare. The only thing in their business that makes any ap- peal to them is the question of a rise in sal- ary and this they rarely endeavour to pro- cure by any original endeavours of their own. ,Sr Albert Spicer said in a recent interview, "If our young fellows are not making pro- gress as tlfey grow with the firm, and become expensive hands for the work they are doing —that is, not qualifying for work of a h'gher leharacter with higher pay—when we come to txamine into the matter for the cause. we xrually discover they are giving. practically ll the:r evenings to pleasure—p.easure, pos- sibly, of quite an innocent kind." This opini- on of a representative man of the great busi- ness world worth serious consideration by those concerned.

• Little. in this life is gained without effort, effort well directed. Success in billiards is no use to a tea-merchant and ability to spot the winner is oftener than not the -high- road to d sasta,r. It would be imnossible to lay down anv hard and fast lines as to how to sp?nd the hours outside the office in such a rway as to be useful from the point of view we have taken. I bank clerk should work for the examinations of the Bankers' Institute. A young friend of mine who entered the stat- istical department of the London County Council was advised by the head of his office to read for a degree in Law at the 17niversity of London. This advice was given on the second: day after the youth entered the ser- vice. He had already been picked out. as one of those who do not mind devoting their leis- ure hours to making themselves efficient. THE MERCHANT'S CLERK.

The merchant's clerk has less need of such definite specialisation but surely there is the world of fore gn languages that is worth ex- ploring, and there are other studies such as commercial geography and , correspondence that we have already mentioned besides eco- nomics, commercial law, and the law of transport. The problem with thesbrain work- er as with the athlete is bow to keep fit. The brain rusts like the muscles and the man with an enfeebled thinking apparatus will mit manage a bank or run a big railway. The present manager of the Great Western Rail- way began at the bottom of the ladder. We may be rare he did not climb by means of the Sing of Spades or any other card in the thumb,marked pack that the clerks spend their time over both morning and evening as they go up to London from the suburbs. THINGS WE HAVE OUTGROWN

- - - --•--• For they can only be unconventional by liv- ing up to certain narrow conventions of their own, the chief of which is that they -must ab- stain from doing those things that, for the general convenience, are done Universally, and SO are not extraordinary. Yet the world has existed so long, and whatever we can do has been done so often before that, as a fact, it is impossible for• anyone to be really uncon- ventional except in suth a petty, local sense as is not worth troubling about'. The uncon- ventionality of to-day is but one of the con- ventionalities of yesterday that the world has wisely outgrown. You can be unconventional nowadays by going regularly to church; but there was a time when everybody went, and then the soar- ing original spirit had to contemn the prac- tice as a commonplace of respectability. You have but to keep on doing anything long- enough and by and by you will pass for un- conventional when the plain truth is you have become nothing more than old- fashioned.

The few old, self-opinionated Britons who continued to go about in a state of nature after the rest of the nation had put on clothes must have been regaracl as thrilling- ly and daringly unconventional; and our jails to-day are full of unconventional fellows who, in the years before laws were invented, would have been recognised as great warriors and heroic conquerors of a usual and quite conventional description. Doubtless there ,were restlessly ambitious beings in those', prehistoric times who ac- quired notoriety and shocked the more re- spectable of our rude forefathers by refusing to wear a proper ooat.of paint, just as in our twentieth century there are tameless and commanding intelleuts, that Will not submit to the tyranny of the top-hat; or they dis- regarded the social usages of their age and nation by being contented _with one wife, or revoltint, against the wearing of sandals, as sirNme modern young men, mostly very young, 'believe they 'demonstrate an originality of Mind by advocating free love, or by donning knee 'breeehea:,-a .rotest against the ugli- ness of trousefs; though the first is a. mere return to a practice that was common in the year .one, and the second is an obsolete con- vention that dates back beyond the Tudor period, It is the minor actress who cannot make a hit without having her jewels stolen, or at- tempting suicide, or getting involved in a sensational divorce case: as it is the inferior worker in any art who is driven to eke out a small talent with bizarre affectations of un- conventionality-. At heart we are all conven- tional, though we will not all admit it.

THE CONVENTIONALITY OF WO—EN. There is an old friend of mine wl,tom I had regarded as a typically orthodox, tractable, highly respectable member of society, and he surprised me one day by announcing in company that he and a certain other one of his guests were very cosmopolitan—that they were the only two present Whose out- look on life was not tiomewhat narrowly con- ventional and suburban. I was surprised be- cause I had never before considered either him or myself in those lights. "What do you mean by suburban ?" I ven- tured.

"oh, well," he said' vaguely, "a man who is really interested in his suburb—who lives the life of his suburb—takes a pride in it, as a patriot does in his country—l should call him ouburban. Now, I am not that and never could be. I sleep in my suburb, but I live in the centre—l can't be contented except in the centre of things where life is at its broadest and its fullest. See ?"

"Aren't you mistaking noise for fullness? I suggested. "I -should have ' thought one would get more light and a broader outlook o:i the circumference than in the centre. But probably I am wrong." And he assured me I was. .

He has married -since then, however; and now he not only lives in what is locally re- garded as the correct style and is a stickler for social etiquette, but he has been elected tc the Borough Council, is a churchwarden, and aspires to become Mayor of his suburb. .Nevertheless, he has not actually altered; he was never unconventional, though he fan- cied he was. He is merely conventional out- side how as well as inside, and those who blame his wife for the revelation are probably right; for woman, in the bulk, is convention- al through and through, and if there is any conventionality in her husband (and, of courEe, there always is) she brings_ it out of him and is not contented till he is fully at- tired in it.

Not that we want her to be unconvention- al; we don't; and she never is though she sometimes tries to be. She will protest to-day against the irksomeness of the marriage bond, and bring an action for breach of pro- mise to-morrow; she will profess belief in platonic affection, and declare that love can- not be constrained and that man or woman should be free to dissolve a partnership the moment one is tired of the other; yet she is unrnsofiably jealous and insists on putting a stop to it if she suspects that her husband is too attentive to somebody else's wife; and if there are doubts whether the married lady residing across the road happens to possess a formal marriage certificate, she bans her from her visiting list and repudiates 11.2 r ac- quaintanoe.

- And who shall blame her? It saves a deal of time and •?nergy to let Society nialx the roads for you to walk in, especially when you remember you however new and wonderful a road you ro.y cut for yourself you will fail, after all, and prove as conventional as the commowst 01 men by dying at the end of it.

When a woman's hat does not suit her the fault is on her own head.

The fact that love makes . the world go o may account for some young folk being rsoir, d' "giddy.

SPRIG OF HEATHER.

Caledonians and Others Sport the '1 Wee Royal " Heath.

IN AID OF CHARITY.

" White heather, sir, a penny a bunch." 'iie voice was that of a Boy Scout doing a "good turn." He was disposing of the "wee royal" sprigs of heath from the Seottish moors at the charity bowling match on Saturday, and the proceeds derived from the pushing, business- like methods of Badeni-nowell's lads went to augment the fund whicli the Works Bowling Association and the Caledonian Association were raising, through a charity match, to aid the Nursing Association and the Children's Summer Camp. . A White Heather Day was a novelty in War- rington, and the Boy Scouts made the most of the innovation to boom their wares. Scotsmen and Lancashiremen vied with each other in sporting the moorland flower.

- Harry- Lauder is responsible for the introduc- tion of a "White Heather Day" into several towns, but who is responsible for the innova- tion in Warrington has not been recorded. The Caledonians doubtless had a considerable finger in the pie, and it was Mr. J. Tennant, the secre- tary of the Caledonian Association, who got the heather.

Real Scotch.

It was a bit of proper Scottish heather that was on sale, not the ling, artificially whitened, so often sold as such it was " the real Mackie," as the Scots say when they wish to imply genuineness. It came from the Hills of Campsie, and Was plucked a short, distance from Falkirk, the Warrington of. 'Scotland. White heather is not unduly plentiful on the Campsie Hills, but it can be got, and what is gathered is genuine, and this on -sale .in :Warrington was real Scotch'.

An interesting romance surrounds the white heather, which has earned it the name of the " wee, royal flower," When the Crown Prince

Photograph by Burrell. HIS WORSHIP BOWLS TO BAGPIPE

MUSIC: -- Mr. T. Locker, Mr. J. Fulton Smith and the Mayor (bowling).

Frederick, father of the German Emperor, was the guest of Queen Victoria at Balmoral, the Boy& Palace in the heart of the Scottish High- lands, the Prince fell in love with la. daughter of the Queen.

The German prince was an affable sort of person, and he talked freely with the servants on the Balmoral estate. One -employe he pet was sporting a sprig of white heatfier, and the Prince was so impressed with the pretty little flower that he 'inquired hill information about it., . . . A Royal Proposal., '

This as readily given, and incidentally he learned that the prebentation of a sprig oi the white heather by a swain to a Scottish lassie was considered a definite proposal of marriage, Interested in this, Frederick asked where the heather was to be found. He was informed, and next day took the Princess for a walk over the moors. Coming to a clump of white grow- ing amidst a dense track of purple heather, he plucked' a sprig and presented it to the: Princess. She accepted it, and a couple of days later the Royal betrothal was officially an- nounced. In this way the heather became popular.

Warrington, on Saturday, did its share in maintaining the popularity of the wild flower. The Boy Scouts sold plentifully for sums some- times that amounted to more than the humble penny. Everybody who professed to be any- bod)" sported a sprig of white heather, and no- body, at least at the Horse and Jockey green, where the match was played, was without it. The Bagpipes.

It was •a real • Scettish . day. The. • players talked Scotch, they performed in a Scotch way, and, whisper it not in Gath, there were-sundry " draps o' Scotch" in evidence. The bagpipes skirled-and droned. Now there were some un- kind folk • who hinted that the bagpipes were brought in order to throw the Works Associa- tion team out of their game, and Mus enable the Scots to play Bannockburn with them, but It proved the reverse, and the Works bowlers fairly made the bowls dance to. 1.7i3 music. They won. How they won and by what majority they won and other particulars of the game itself can be read on page 9. The Mayor, with a .sprig of heather, and the accompanying skirl of the pipes to make him a thorough Caledonian, led off the 'game, and at the close of play the Scots added fresh spice to the match by an exhibition of the ".Ilighland fling," the sword dance and other national reels. The pipe music • and the dancing was chiefly contributed. by the Stoddart family, who came from Seacombe to participate in the interesting function.

The Shopkeeper's Trick.

' Shopkeeper (to new boy): "When you've finished sweeping the shop and put things straight, don't hang about wasting your tithe. You can be catching flies and putting them into our new patent fly-trap, so that it will be ready to put in the window,"

DEATH OF MR. WM. ARNOLD

I Successful and Honoured Colborne Pastor.

FORMER STOCWTON HEATH RESIDENT.

We deeply regret to record the death of Mr.. William Arnold, formerly a well-known resi- dent of Stockton Heath, and latterly pastor of the Baptist Church at Golborne. Mr. Arnold was the only son, of the late Mr. William. Arnold, stonemason, of Stockton Heath, and it is interesting to record that he was the sixth generation of those bearing the same name and following the same business. The early home of the family was at_ Witten, Northwich, but the last two or three genera- tions have lived in .Stockton Heath.

About five years ago, feeling himself drawn towards the ministry, Mr. Arnold took charge of the Baptist Mission Church at Colborne, and shortly afterwards gave up his business to de- vote himself entirely to that work. His early religious associations were with the Hill Cliffe Baptist Church, where he held the office of deacon, and at one period Sunday school superintendent. His work at Colborne has been very successful, and he won for him- self a high place in the esteem of all the churches.

After several months of indifferent health, he succumbed to an attack of pneumonia on Satur- day last. He was in his 42nd year, and leaves a widow and one• son, for whom great sympathy is felt.

THE FUNERAL.

At the funeral, which took place at Hill Cliffe graveyard on Tuesday, there was remarkable evidence of the esteem in which he has been held at Golborne. About one hundred mem- bers of his congregation and of other local churches foll9wed ti'M hearse in conveyances, and others travelled by rail and cycle. An impressive service was conducted at pOl- borne Baptist Church by the Rev. H. W. Callow (Congregational minister). There was a very large attendance. The hymns "Eternal light" and "For ever with the Lord" were sung. . The. Rev.. H. W. CALLOW said a true hero, a saint and a brother beloved, had been taken from them, and We 'forces of righteousness in their township had been greatly weakened. His one aim in life was to follow in the footsteps of the Lord. People of all denominations had the utmost confidence in him, and he was beloved by all who, knew him. In .his concluding re- marks, Mr. Callow recalled his friend's last ser- mon, in which he' said, "If the Lord were to call me at this moment lam ready." They were all in true sympathy with the widow and her son.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
SERVICE AT HILL CLIFFE.

A largely attended service was held at Hill Cliffe Chapel prior to the interment. The Rev. 'George Curtis (pastor) officiated, and the Rev. H. W. Callow was also present. The hymr "Servant of God, well done I" was sung., The Rev. G. Cum.'s, who was much moved, said he had only known Mr. Arnold two years, yet during that time he had found huh to be a genuine Christian man, a loving pastor and friend. He would tell the Colborne people that he (the late Mr. Arnold) thought highly of them, and of the church. They worked together in real unity, and the work was a success. It would, he ventured to say, be a long time before they found such another. He was a hard worker,. and a keen organiser, and they would feel That a noble servant of Christ had been called to his reward.

The organist, Miss Grounds, played. some fit- ting selections: The hymn "Rock of ages "was sung at the graveside. The chief nkr:rners were: Master W. Canadians In London.

Canadians. rarge numbers are attending the services at the City Temple, London, where Canada's popular preacher, the Rev. John McNeil, MA., of Toronto, is taking the place of the Rev. A. J. Campbell, ILA., during the holiday season. Mr. McNeill is one of the most prominent of the young men of the Baptist Church of Canada, and is gifted with rare elo- quence and power. This is the second occasion on which Mr. Campbell has asked the Toronto pastor to conducfi the services at the City Temple, which will always be associated with the ministry of the renowned Dr. Joseph Parker.

Wycliffe P.S.A

There was a good meeting on Sunday, presided over by Mr. B. Farrar, who referred to the golden wedding of the Rev. John Yonge and Mrs. Yonge, which took place this week. They were grateful, he said, for all the services Mr. Yonge had given to the P.S.A. work. It was decided to send a letter of congratulation to Mr. and Mrs. Yonge, wishing for them many happy years to come, with peace at eventide. Mr. H. Wardman gave the address, upon "What is lifer' The pessimist, he said, stated that life was suffering, trouble and unrest- things happened which were to happen, and they charged God with causing these things, when, as a fact, it was men who broke governing laws and had to suffer in consequence. The machinery was good for its mechanical designs, but when these designs crossed, mischief was done and accidents happened. Let us use gov- erning laws, obey God's will, and we shall work out good to ourselves, good to others, and life will be not solely suffering, but peace, help and joy. We Shall then realise life is worth Hying. Mri Mcßee-hem also took nart in the meeting. Mr. R. Wallington presided at the organ. A happy and interesting meeting closed with the Benediction.

IN THE COURTS.

BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

MONDAY.

Before the Mayor (in the chair), Aldermen Smethurst, James Evans and Bolton, Messrs. Pilling, W. Roberts, W. Furness and T. Locker. CHALLENGED ANYBODY TO FIGHT,---ThOmas Man- nion, 109, Winwick-road, was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Winwick-street on Saturday night.—Mr. Roberts, landlord of the Bay Horse Inn, said Mannion went into his house and was served before his condition was noticed. The drink was immediately taken away and the money returned as soon as it was seen he was in drink. He then started to sing and was ordered to leave the premises. On refusing to go he was ejected, and Mannion then struck him on the side and scratched. his hand. —Thomas Knight, barman, said Mannion struck him repeatedly in the struggle. The police came and found accused stripped and challeng- ing anybody to fight him.—The Mayor said the case was a bad one, but the Bench would give Mannion a chance.—Fined 10s. and costs. CEMETERY AS DRINK CURE.—John Heaton, a local character, was charged with having been found drunk and disorderly id the Market-place.— Heaton admitted the offence and said the only time he could keep sober was when he was in gaol for three months. They might keep him away from the drink if they sent him to prison for six months, or laid him in the Cemetery. (Laughter.) He, however, now promised not to touch more drink and was fined ss. or seven days in prison.

HELD UP A TRAM.—Walter Payne and Fred Davies were fo4nd drunk and disorderly .in Knutsford-roadron Saturday night.—A constable said they stood on the tram lines and refused to get off, with the result that a tram was held up.—Fined 58. and costs each, with the option of seven days.

INJURED HER. HUSDAND.—Elizabeth Chilton, a married woman, of 7, Rodney-street, was charged with using obscene language and with assaulting her husband.—Superintendent Wil- liamson said when the police arrived they found the husband with a cut eyebrow, which had to be stitched at the Infirmary, and later the woman went into the street and used had lan- guage.—The husband said his eyebrow was cut with a tin can his wife threw at him.—The accused was fined 55., with the option of a week in _prison._ .

AT SAD Ctse.—Mary Harris, 19, Bemick-street, was .charged .with having been drunk and dis- orderly.—P.C. Ballard said she was behaving most disorderly when he took her in charge.— Inspector Foster stated that when taken to the Police Station, Harris looked as if she had been dragged through a hedge.—lt was stated that every effort had been made to reform the woman, and the Mayor, who characterised it as a sad case, imposed a fine of ss. or seven days.

TUESDAY. Before Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes and T. Locker. Hs GOT LODOINGS.—Goorge Alexander, no fixed abode, was sent to`prison for seven days for beg- ging. To a constable he said he was begging for money to get lodgings. The officer added that he gave him a night's lodgings at the Police Station. He said he caMe "from a place called Chester."

THE WATERCRESS SELLER.—Edward Finn, water- cress seller, was found drunk in Bridge-street, with a basket of cress strapped to his shoulder. —Finn pleaded for a small fine, as he had 10s. worth of watercress, which, he said, would be spoilt if he was sent to prison. As soon as he sold the cress he would pay the fine and he would go straight to the priest and sign the pledge. "I am teetotal." he added." sometimes for six months on end."—Superintendent Wil- liamson said there were 46 previous convictions against him.—Finn got a chance and was fined ss. and costs.

THURSDAY.

Before Mr. A. J. Jolley (in the chair), Alderman Bolton, Messrs. W. Roberts, J. Upson, W. T. Williams, and Dr. Naden.

A PITIABLE PLlGHT.—Ragged and unkempt! John Lawrie, no fixed abode, presented a pitiable spectacle when charged with wandering. He said he had slept out since he left the Infirmary after an accident, and Superintendent William- son stated that Lawrie spent the nights on the river bank.—Mr. Browne (solicitor) said Lawrie seemed to have lost his memory since the acci- dent. He had a claim for compensation for Lawrie in hand, but could not get him. to sign papers, or do anything. Lawrie often rang up the housekeeper at the office at five o'clock in the morning to ask if he (Mr. Browne) was in. Up till four months ago Lawrie had been a respectable and a good workman.—He was re- manded to the Workhouse for 14 days.,

WHEN WOMEN FALL OUT.—Joan Lewis and Martha Littler were summoned for fighting in Thelwall-lane, where they reside.—P.C. Hayes said when he arrived upon the scene the place was in an uproar. Lewis blamed Littler for starting the fight and 'Littler blamed Lewis.— Superintendent Williamson said Lewis, who had been six times previously before the magis- trites, would never give her neighbours peace.— Littler was ordered to pay 3s. 6d.. the cost of the summons, and Lewis was fined 10s. 6d., including costs. ,

Other witnesses esti- mated the speed at thirty miles, but defendant denied that and said the car could not do more than 15 miles.—The ease was dismissed on pay- ment of .21 3s. ed. costs.

TEE BAILIFF'S LOT.—A bailiff's lot is not ft pleasant one, as Arthur Gallop, of 34: Dial- street, experienced on Monday, when Ile was assaulted by George Turner, a carter, of 80, Hume-street. The sequel was heard at the Police Court on Friday. when Turner was sum- moned for assault.—Gallop said that he was en- gaged by Mr. R. Lowe. estate agent, to visit the house of the defendant. He got into the house, and when defendant Cala(' home to his dinner. he struck him 'several times on the head, and put witness outside the house. Defendant denied striking Gallop, but admitted putting him outside.—Mr. Lowe said it was through such men as the defendant that they had a difficulty in getting men to act as hailiffs.—A fine of tis. or seven days was imposed. the Mayor remarking that it was against thv law to turn out the bailiff when in possession.

A MISTAKEN APPEAL.

£lOO,OOO FOR ATHLETES WHO WILL COMPETE FOR APPLAUSE AGAINST

BE-RIBBONED STALLIONS.

Say what its friends will, the .appes.l :for £lOO,OOO to train competitors for the so-called Olympic Games, to be held at Berlin in 1916, is a mistaken one. The public is beginning to have an uneasy feeling that its pockets are con- stantly being picked by a new and clever school. Fagin now sits in the editorial chair, and he trains his dear young friends how to whip a hundred guineas or a fiver or even ix nimble half-crown from a private pocket with such light fingers that it is not missed. Charley Bates and the Artful Dodger have changed their togs, but not their habits. But there are other sides to the question. The "Times" summarise', the. objects of the fund :-- (1) To enable. Britain to play her proper part in the Olympic Games at Berlin in 1916, and to regain the prestige lost at Stockholm in 1912. (2) To encourage amateur sports and athletics. (3) To raise the standard of physical effi-

ciency of the youth of the nation: - Who of us is conscious that Britain lost pres- tige at Stockholm, • unless indeed the word is used in its original sense signifying "delusion"? If indeed StockhOn witnessed a British dis- grace, then the nation must in this autumn of her history be simply smothered in the dust of humiliation after Wimbledon this summer and Meadowbank. Why use big words for small events? Olympic Games is a purely catchpenny title r these international sports are less like the ancient Greek games in spirit or influence than the Macedonia of the present time resem- bles the Macedon that,Corcebus knew.

Is there any real necessity to encourage ama- teur sports and athletics in Britain at the pre- sent time? We cannot discern it. It has been dinned into our ears generation after generation that the curse of British education is the fetish it makes of athleticism. Thirdly, Will the pro- posed training raise the standard of physical efficiency of the youth of the• nation—only the male youth, be it understood? The female youth is not to be trained to balance heavy waterpots on the shoulder and thus acquire new grace of carriage. A stranger would imagine _ _

that athletics are not practised to-day at the public schools and the universities; that there do not exist such institutions as naval and mili- tary gymnasia. Are the athletic displays which so many of us have witnessed with genuine de- light at the Military Tournament a forgotten memory, or have they no value? The rear object of the fund is to create a small gladia- torial " family " of athletes, who after Berlin will, we stippose, be sent round to compete for the plaudits, of the crowd at Bank' Holiday. shows with prize bulls Rad be-ribboned stal- lions.—" The World."

THE PATRIOTS.

We are making headway in our ideas of patriotism and constitutional Governz-Nent. That section of, the Conservative party that t.ls taken the -protection of Ulster under its wing is showing us what those words sometimes mean. They are patriots before all else, With the result' that we find a letter admitted to the columns of a newspaper that is looked upon as representing the Orange side of Northern Ire- land, using sh choke phrases as "Let KING. GEORGE sign the Home Rule Bill and he is ,no. longer my King," and "1 am going to see KING GEOFIG2 in person at Buckingham Palace, and I will tell, him 'lf you sign, the Home Rule Bill YOU will 10Se your crown and Empire.": Three- parts of Ireland has demanded Home Rule for generations, and at two General Elections the nation, as a whole, has admitted the justice of that claim, but the minority still wants its own. way and threatens even the KING if it cannot, get its own way. Long before Sir EDWARD CARSON'S UlstPr• Campaign was concluded, the Tory Press grew tired of It. But the Tory journals have tried to create a sensation amongst the public, which, is altogether un- moved by. Sir • EDWARD CARSON'S " Vlsterics," with kinemacoloured descriptions of Protestant Ulster as an 'armed Camp. The latest thing in the flesh-creeping line from Ulster is the announcement of "An Advisory Board for the Oster Volunteer Force, • appointed at head- %letters." Wooden guns have been superseded by a parlour Army Council. The position of the leaders of the Tory party in face of this make-believe revolution is distinctly peculiar. Sir EDWARD CARSON and his lieutenants frankly avow that it would not affecf their resistance to Home Rule if the Bill were submitted to another election, or any number of General Elections, and approved by overwhelming 'niajorities of voters. But the Tory ledders declare that they would give no emintenance to Ulster resistance if the Horne Rule Bill were app-roved at another General • Election. Yet they have admitted, from time to time. that there is nothing but apathy in the constituencies of Great Britain when the Ulster bogey is presented to them. The Tory party want to try their luck at another Cendral Election, and they are willing to fan the smouldering flames of religious or racial antagonism in Ireland as a reckless means to a sordid end. Having failed in their "gamble With the food. of the rpeople," they are now trying an equally ridiculous and reckless, Plunge.

The Scotsman's Only Concern. A visitor, peacefully flounder fishing off the shore of Arran, found his punt suddenly tear- ing through the water at a speed. that would have been alarming even in a motor boat. He rushed to the bow, and found his anchor had been caught up by a submarine. As it. was impossible to recover the anchor, and as ,the boat was in some danger of swamping, .he cut the rope and, in a state of some excitement, rowed ashore to tell the boat-owner all about it. The Scotsman was "most unusual calm." When the story was finished fie gave two or three "Aye-ayes," and remarked, "And so ye lost the anchor? That'll be ten shullins."

Something Like Holiday Form. After the hustle and bustle of English trains and railway stations (writes a correspondent in the "Manchester City News"), the leisurely manner of the Isle of Man, railway system came to me as a surprise, and 'a pleasant surprise for one who loves to take his holidays lazily. The time-table, I found, was merely a list of guesses at the holm' of arrival or departure of trains, though it must be said that the table gave at length reasons for the uncertainty. However, never by any chance did I find a train start before its time, and almost invariably it was anything from five to thirty minutes late. The trusting nature. of the railway servants was also Impressed upon me. I asked for a ticket at a wayside station one day and tendered half- a-sovereign. The fare was 3s. 7d., and the sta- tionmaster, who happened to be the booking clerk as well, shook his head when I offered the coin and regretted that he could not change it. I explained that I had only 2s. 7d. in small change. " Oh, well," he said, `: give HIP that. The other shilling will do the 'next time you see me."

WHO SHALL BE MAYOR?

This, one of the most intereSting questions asked during the municipal year, is being put in various quarters. I,Ve understand that it \vitt be officially raised at a meeting of the Council on Tuesday evening, held immediately before the ordinary monthly business is taken. in recent years the fashion of electing .and re- electing the Mayor for three successive years has been followed with the best of results. Dr. Joseph is just finishing his third term of office, following immediately upon three .years each by ' Alderman Forsliaw, Alderman Smethurst, and ,Alderman Bolton—which takes us, back to 1903. For the two years prior to that date Mr. J. C. Parr Served as an outside mayor. Alder- man Henry Roberts had been Mayor for the years 1899-1900-1901, which shows us That we have not had a Liberal Mayor since the opening year of the Ventury, now in its teens. To dis- cover aiiother Libtiral occupant of the Mayoral Chair, we must go back To 1882-3, when, for a year, the late Mr. John Crosfield bore the duty and discharged I,he dignities of the office. If we go further back still. to the time when the town was incorporated in 1847, and. our celebrated townsman, Mr. William Beam Ont, was. first Mayor, we shall find the records, containing the names of Conservatives and Liberals in about the same proportion as during the last 30 years, when Mr. 'John Crosfield- and Me. Henry Roberts are the only- ones to break the dull monotony of Conservative domination. "Domination" .may perhaps be too strong a term to apply over the. whole of the period, because there have been times when, circum- stances "have made it impossible for 'Liberals to accept the post when it appeared probable that it would be offered to them. But these occasions have been rare. In any case, the Liberals of the town think that it is about time one of their number occupied the chair; and, if those who represent the party are prepared with a candidate to accept the honour this year, we hope the Council as a whole-Ahat is; not only the Liberals, but the Conservatives and the Independents—will see the justice of their claim and unanimously acquiesce. There can be no doubt, as we have shown, about the reasonable- ness of this suggestion.

THE .RECORD MAYOR.

Only one gentleman appears to have been Mayor of Warrington for more than three years. This was the late Major John Riaiard Pickmere, who was Mayor for three consecutive years, 1879-804-2. This gentleman had already been Mayor in 18734, and on Dr. Mackie's retirement, in the midst of the 1886-7 mayorAity, Major Pickmere was chosen to complete the term, so that he had, altogether, nearly five years at the helm. He was admitted a Freeman of the borough in 1894, and died in 1896. There are ten names on our Freemen's roll, only two of whom—Mr. J. C. Parr and Mr. Robert Pierpoint —are alive.

• OCR CARS AND OUR ELECTRICITY. There is. a general feeling locally that. our municipal electric and tramways undekakings are doing well, but it is essential in these, as in other matters, that we should see ourselves, occasionally, as detached observers see us. We are given this opportunity this week by the "Electrical Times," which analyses .the accounts of these two closely-connected departments with candour and completeneSs. The supply of electricity has continued to increase,' and the net result of going through a maze Of figures is ,the conclusion that in the last financial year there remained a surplus ,of £2,944, "a highly satisfactory return," considering that it repre- sented not only an increase cif'l42oo upon the, previous .year's surplus, but also an increased .percentage of profit upon invested capital of 8.71., against 7.44—gross. We .fancy we detect a sue4ges!ion af the dissatisfaction,that has been expressed at different times in town regarding the large proportion of the output that is used for power, and the small charge relatively that is made to manufacturers for that part of the supply. 'Power supplies, it is pointed out in an emphatic paragraph, represent the- bulk of the business and account for 85 per cent, of the sales to private consumers. The average re- ceipts "consequently touch a low level, being no higher than 1.34 d. per unit:" but the effect on the financial position has been "relieved" by a saving in the working costs. Of course, the same argument applies to supplying elec- tricity at cheap -.rates to manufacturers as to allowing them to send out smoke, but with far more reason. Cheap power encourages trade, and busy workshops employ the ratepayers, directly and indirectly.

ARE WE BECcorNpNO_ MORE Lin?

Tile' electric cars,. VVhicli have now been 'run- ning. eleven years, increased the number of miles they ran from 443,560 to 449,206 on the year. This is not a big increase in the number of journeys. btit the cars were so much better patronised that 13.50 passengers were carried per'car mile, against 12.73 in the previous year, while the revenue per car mile went up from 11.55 d. to 12.23 d. As the costs ascenNd only .68d. the outlook was exceedingly bright, but heavier repairs largely absorbed these extra profits, and all things considered, the final surplus of £2,982 on, the year's working, an increase of about £5OO, was highly satisfactory —from a trading point of view. But there is another point of view. Does this extra use of the ears mean •that we are becoming .more' lazy in our 'habits,. as is sometimes stated?, We may be groWing 'less inci ined to -walk when we -bee an easy thearis:of•riding placed at our disposal, but this does not altogether account- for the growing popularity of the trams. More people are living on the outskirts of the town than ever, in search of fresh air, and pleasanter sur- roundings than its centre can offer, and trade is good. There is much evidence, in fact, that the growth of tram business is a healthy growth in every respect. The departments over whieli Alderman Smethurst presides,' so assiduously, are worthy of all praise. •

"Do WE GRUNIFILF: Too MUCH?"

The following appeared in the " Daily News ". this week

Sir,--Ought we to grumble? Undoubtedly not! Our song should rather be one of rejoic- ing. As a nation we are particularly blessed. We have a splendid country, with great natural resources—greater than most people are aware of—and, thanks to our wonderful geographical position, we are practically the sea gateway to the:whole world.. Moreover, our much-abused climate is an -exceptionally healthy One; as Is proved. by .the fact that our death-rate is the lowest, of all the leading civilised nations. Why, even London's death-rate, vast city as it is, is much lower than that of New York, Paris, or -Berlin.

Legislatively, too, we are proceeding on the right lines, for the large majority of the nation is naturally, thanks to education, progressive.

E. ENGLAND.

Warrington. Aug. 25.

Yet sore Liberals have been under the im- pression that Mr. England did not believe that all was right with the world. Mr. England, we know, was always on the side 8f progress, and we are glad of the assurance that legisla- tively we are proeeding on the right lines.
