INSURANCE.

The Amendment Act Comes into Operation Next Monday.

SOME OF THE IMPROVEMENTS. How a Great Measure Is being Perfected

[" Examiner" Special.]

A great deal of misrepresentation followed in the wake of the National Insurance Act of 1911.

When It was introdticed as a Bill it was in- stantly recognised as the greatest effort at social reform that this country had ever witnessed, and candid Conservatives volun- teered the statement that it meant the exclu- sion of their party from power for years, if not permanently. This was why the campaign of calumny was begun. If the Insurance Bill was not black- ened in the eyes of the party, it was, they concluded, "Goodbye" to Tariff Reform and other .specifics the Unionist party had for keeping the people quiet, bolstering up the cause of Ulster faction, and saving the House I of Lords.

The Governineni simply set their teeth, Mid, having lived down the temporary effects of the interested abuse from which they had suf- fered, they proceeded to make die-necessary amendments in the light of actual experience. The Amendment Act of 1913 has now become law. H amends_ in several important parti- culars the Act of 1911, and most of its provi- sions come into operation next Monday, September Ist. Some are deferred to sub- sequent 'dates between September Ist and 7 January 15th.

An " Examiner " representative had an interesting interview With a 'gentleman well versed in the Act, and the changes the amend- ments will effect, • ." What, in your opinion, are the most important alterations?" he asked.

The Wisdom of Early Action.

"To begin with," was the reply, "the period during' which a person is entitled to •enter into insurance as an employed contributor at the ordinary rate and to receive full benefits is ex- tended until October 12th, 1913.

"The period within which persons may enter into insurance as voluntary contributors at lower rates .of contribution is also extended until October 12th. Any persons who are not employed within the meaning of the At, but engaged in some regular occupation and wholly or mainly dependent .for their livelihood on the earnings - derived from that occupation, are entitled to become voluntary contributors, pro- vided their income is less than £l6O, and such peoplo• are advised to- take immediate advan- tage of ,this concession. " lishould like," he added, ." to draw attention to the wisdom. under:thiS head, of early action.. There are complicated sliding Scales of pep merit; but I .may lake two cases for the riklm- poses of comparison. At present the contrii- bUtioh 'of a man of 45 is 70.; and of a woman the' same, age (Id. After October 13th, a gradu- ated table will come -into fore wider which men'at 45 will pay 101 d. and women 9id.

Continued Illness and Reduced Benefits.

" Two .highly important. provisions are those which remove a continued illness hardship, and abolish the reduction of benefits.

"The requirement of Section 8 (5) of the original Act, that 50 contributions should be paid between the end of one illness and the commencement of another, in order that the second should not count as a. continuation of the first, is removed. "As from the beginning of the next quarter, Okober 13th, there is no longer any reduction of sickness benefit of employed contributors who entered insurance at the age of 50 or up- wards. Therefore until arrears :penalties conic into operation the rate of sick pay for all con- tributors of 21 years will be 10s.. per week for men and 7s. 6d. for women..

Maternity Benefits htereased. "The new Act includes a provision which comes into force in October, designed ,to secure that a •Woman shall in no cti:se be worse ,off, because her husband is insured than she would be if he were not insured. This provision requires that if the husband is a member of a society, but (through the insufficiency of his contributions, or.on account of arrears) is not entitled to maternity benefit, the woman's society shall pay maternity benefit in addition to sickness benefit, as if he were not insured.

, • "Commencing January 12th maternity benefit is in every ease to be the mother's benefit, andt if paid to the husband, it is his duty to pay it ro his wife. Instead of any sickness benefit for four weeks. after confinement; an insured -mar- ried Woman employed A contributor is • entitled to receive a second 'maternity benefit on the 'condition' that she .abstains from remunerative work for foui-weckS after her confinement."

Doctor's Fee and the Maternity Benefit. • According •to the amended Act, the doctors fee will no longer be recoverable from the maternity benetit, said our representative, wish- ing to emphasise a point which has caused sonic little trouble in the country. In answer to a precautionary' sentence as to rthe advisability of saying anything that might annoy the meth.- cal geritlenien, the "-Examiner" man yepliel that no such question troubled his mind. The Act was the Act for which Parliament, and not the interviewer, was responsible, and it was well both for the public and the doctors to know exactly ‘how the land ties under the altered con- ditions.

" Well," was the brief anawer, "Section 18 (1) of the principal Act ceases to have effect. That section provided that the fee of a doctor called in to a confinement by a midwife in accordance with the rules under the Midwives Act, 1902, should be recoverable as part of the maternity benefit; but now the maternity benefit may be paid in full as soon as the society has evidence of the confinement."

Less Work for Secretaries. "Commencing January 12th, the first six-

monthly card %ill be issued. This will neces- sdate alterations in the registers, and will con- sequently* somewhat lighten the heavy burden of ,the. secretaries of " numerous branches of approved societies."

Post Once Contributors. • This clogs of contributor, who represent the weakest link in the insurance chain, came up for discussion.

Going to the Post Office, 'where there are relatively no benefits," said the insurance 'ex- pert, "is merely d matter of sentiment. Men getting less than ,tl6O a year, who do not wish it to be known that they get:, less, go, as the' think, privately, to the Post Office, but they for- get that their cases have to be dealt with, by a local committee, and in other ways. • • • • • - The points dealt with here are but a few of many bearing . upon a .well-considered -attempt to make the original measure approach more nearly to that state of perfection which it is the intention of the Government it shall attain. Readers will be able to obtain the Act, and make themselves conversant with these latest condi- tions of 'a measure which has undoubtedly justified its existence and claim to provid&r for insurance against loss of health and for the prevention and cure of sickness.

AMATEUR DIVER.

Work at the Bridge Recalls an "Examiner" Writer's Adventure. ALONE IN THE SEA BOTTOM.

The presence of a diver at Warrington Bridge recalls the part played by an "Examiner" representative as an amateur diver. His adventures in the bottom of the sea took place at a popular seaside resort, and he spent some fifteen minutes under the water. The experience proved instructive, exciting and somewhat amusing, but not so amusing at the time he was undergoing a deep sea thrill.

Walking alone at the bottom of the sea may be a common experience with the diver, as the song says. To the amateur who ventures upon the task it is a time of thrills. I have been in some ,queer adventures in my time, but for sheer excitement nothing equalled that Which I underwent when I spent•some fifteen minutes on the sea. floor. My then experience has been recalled Or the presence of a diver at War- rington" Bridge, but venturesome as I am, the murky waters of the .Mersey would not tempt me to descend there; I chose as the scene of my diving experience a popular seaside resort. A friend of mine, Andrew Cameron, the holder of the world record for deep sea diving, and a man who has fought and killed the shark, octopus, and other marine Monsters on the ocean floor in different parts of the world, finally 'losing a leg in a -battle with a huge, shark at Havana in. Cuba: .*,ve, engaged repair- ing the s6a resort pier, With some little dill"- cultY. I persuaded him to let me • descend in his company. His permission obtained, I com- menced to prepare for the submarine journey.. Getting Ready. First I donned a • thick flannel jersey, and then the heavy rubber diving suit. Over this was placed the corselet, and to my feet were attached the leaded clogs. The copper helmet was screwed on, and the life-line, as well as the air tube, properly secured. After a jocular farewell, my companion fixed the glass mouth- piece to the helmet and left me completely en- cased in the diving suit. Even then my equip- ment was scarcely complete. The attendant or tender, as he is called, applied the finishing touch in the shape of two heavy weights, which he strapped over, my •shoulders. In all I now carried something like 1801 b., a weight neces- sary to counteract the buoyancy of the body when in the, water:

At a sigmil from Cameron, I commenced my descent into the depths of the ocean, the tender meanwhile carefully playing out the air pipe and life-line. Slowly and with difficulty, I went down the ladder into the water. A few steps and I felt a marvellous change. The heavy weight, which pressed upon me when I stood on the pier, 'seemed to be instantly're- moved, and I felt as if I Carried a much less handicap weight than 180Ib. Half-way down I halted to take observations. Above I could see the staging; below I could scarcely discern the outline of the ladder.. Descending further, my eyes became more accustomed to the surround- ing gloom, and I was able to make out objects more clearly. At last I reached the foot. of the ladder, where I was joined by Cameron. There in the sea bottom, twenty-five feet below the surface, we stood hand in hand. It was an exciting moment for Me. A. Weird Scene.

The scene-around was weird in the extreme. Resting on the shell-strewn sandy bottom were ;the iron cOlumns supporting the pier above. ihese were completely hidden by shell fish !piled above each other in fantastic•array, while the long waving seaweed suggested the idea of isk.)che huge, fierce creature with numerous long !wavy tentacles, ready,. to seize anything that might come within its grasp. As the idea en- tered my mind it sent a tremour through my body, and a,tug at something around my waist forced me- to imagine that I was within the clutch of my conjured up monster. A still more violent puli, ho#ever, . brought me back to my proper senses., Then I remembered the code of cOmmunication signals, and giving an answering pull to tOe life-line, I signalled to the tender above that all Was well. '

Under my companion's guidance, I com- menced my walk. As we moved forward our clogs stirred up the liquid mud, which hovered around us like a yellow cloud. The silence was intense, and it was a decided relief to hear even the faint beating of the the air pump above. On we travelled until the particular part of the foundations where Cameron wat, then at work was reached. In dumb show, he explained the nature and method of his work. A Shark—Nfot Quae. • Craduvy I was becoming familiar with the work, and was inclined to flatter myself upon my pluck, When, to my horror; I perceived two bright eyes peering at me through the yellow Cloud.of,niud surrounding us. The eyes at once suggested the presence of a shark, and every second I expected to find myself engulfed in the creature's jaws. I clutched my com- panion's hand in terror, as the vivid. details of some of his brevious adventures with sharks flashed through my mind. With a violent shake my companion brought me back to my senses.

Then I observed that, the cause of my fear was not the dreaded .shark but a. small fish belonging to the cod species.. Such is the power of imagination. The fish was quite' tame, and eagerly deVoured a meal Of shell fish, prepared • :by. - diver, with whom it. seemed on most friendly terms.

We- were now on-the return journey, and when crossing -a patch of sand something fell heavily upon my shoulders and bore me to the sand. The first impression was that I had been struck by a sinking Dreadnought, but I was soon Undeceived as my companion rolled over my: prostrate figure. However, he promptly lifted me to my feet, and soon we reached the ladder and the surface. Willing hands removed the weights and helmet, as I sunk into .a seat -well-night exhausted with my adventures in the deep. My companion had also stripped off his gear., and laughingly con- gratulated me upon my pluck in Acing such a voracious shark.

"And," he asked, "how did you like the game of leap frog as played Under water." Then .the reason .for his knocking me down became ap- parent, but I was forced to pocket my indigna- tion when he exPlained that all young divers had ,to undergo such an ordeal. It was the initiation ceremony into the craft.

A Strange Adventure.

".Your fright over the imagined shark,". con- tinued Cameron, "is but one of the many. that young divers are subjected to. I have had my ghare of them. The greatest and most realistic fright I ever experienced was while at work on a sunken. mailboat just • off Ceylon, and seeing you don't mind a yarn, I'll tell you about it. Among the passengers drowned was the daughter of a Government official, and I was specially. instructed to search for her body. For a long time I failed to find a trace of it. As a last resource I searched the staterooms. "Slightly opening one of the doors I perceived the young lady sitting on a chair or stool. I pulled open the door, and' imagine my .horror when the corpse seemed to rise and advance towards me. The sight was too much, and, rushing from the. spot, I gave the signal ,to be hauled to the Snrface. "Some time afterwards J Went dowligain, and found that the suction caused by the open- ing of the cabin door had made the corpse float towards me. the shock this weird incident gave me cannot be described in words. It took years before the ghostly scene was banished from- thy. mind, and it was revived—you can believe this di' not, just as you like—in a most startling manner when I was in Regent-street, London, on,one occasion.

"I was about to sail for a .diving loin in Australia, and was killing time in London. was sauntering along Regent-street when a (Continued in mit column.)

Milk to Cost Iron'More.

The milk is likely to cost more. The whole- sale dealers after Michaelmas propose to raise the price from a farthing to a halfpenny a *gallon; 'consequent 'upon- the: increiased railway rates and the higher cost of commodities.

Knocked Down by a Waggon. Daniel Cannon, a labourer, employed by. Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. at their Wil- derspool Brewery, was, on Monday, knocked down by a railway waggon. lie received injuries which necessitated his removal to the Infirmary.

An Interesting Sale. An interesting sale of valuable furniture and pictures, part of the estate of the late Miss Emily Jane Massy, takes place at Greenbank, Latchford, on Monday and Tuesday. The auctioneers are Messrs. T. Sutton and Sons.

A New Congregational School Chapel. The work of erecting the new Congregational School-Chapel In Elinwood,avenue, Padgate- lane, is nearly completed, and the opening cere- mony has been fixed for Thursday, 11th Septem- ber, when it is expected the Rev. James Cregan, the Rev. A: J. Viner, the Rev. T. T. James. the Rev. -John Yonge and others will take part in the interesting function.

"Long Live the King" Author Dead. The author of " Long live the King," which was sung by the girls at Messrs. Crosfiel4's Works when the King and Queen visited them during the Royal visit in July, died suddenly at Buxton. Mr. Maybriek, who wrote under the name of "Stephen Adams," was a native of Liverpool, and brother of the victim of the famous Maybrick case of some 30 years ago.

• Women's Co-operative Guild. A meeting was held on Tuesday in the Co- operative hall, Cairo-street, under the auspices of the Warrington Equitable 'lndustrial Co- operative Society, with the object of forthing a branch of the Women's Co-operative -Guild in -connection with the society. Mrs. Billinge and 311 s. • %'arrington Man4Dies in Gaol. A Warrington man who was sent to prison for two months died during the week-end in Strangeways, Manchester. An inquest was held on Monday. it was stated that the man was Luke Hanwright, who had been convicted in the Borough Court.—Major Nelson, the governor of the gaol, stated that the man was received into the prison on August 15th from Warrington borough, having been sentenced to two months' hard labour under the Vagrancy Act. He was in good health i on reception, but was admitted to hospital on the 17th, and died on the 22nd.. Death was stated to be due to pneumonia fol- lowing influenza. A 'verdict of "Death from natural causes" was returned. .

• Warrington Win at Water Polo. -The Warrington water polo team scored a de- cisive victory over Seacombe and Egremont in the Corporation Baths, Legh-street, on Thurs- day evening. From the throw-in the visitors attacked persistently and Woods was called upon to save three shots from short range. The game underwent a startling change, for War- rington were six goals up in about as many minutes. Ince was the first to pierce the defence, and his example was followed by Peers (two), Turner (two), and Sisson, the visiting goalkeeper having little chance of saving the shots. At the interval Warritgton led by six goals to nil. The Second half was m9re rvnly -contested. After Peers mid sc:)red :loin for Warrington, the 'visitors, through Dovan. put on a couple of goals, the final being seven goals to two in Warrington's favour. In the 50 yards club handicap the following won their heats : R. Hewitt, W. O'Niel, R. Pepper and G. Aloly- neux. . In the final W. O'Neil. was first home, followed closely by Hewitt and Pepper.

Death of Mr. Arthur Dennett.

It is with regret that we announce the death of Mr. Arthur -Bennett, of Widnes, and formerly of Warrington, which took place last week-end, after a very brief illness. The late. Mr. Dennett, who was 44 years of age, and Inid been employed by the L. and N.-W. Railway Company for twenty years, leaves a widow and three daugh- ters and one son, The interment took place at Widnes 'Cemetery on Tuesday, four colleagues acting as bearers. Among the Mourners were the widow and family, Mrs. E. G. Hughes and Mrs. E. Birchall (sisters), Messrs. Walter and Alfred Dennett (brothers), Messrs. William, Walter and Reggie Dennett (nephews) ; Mr. and Mrs. Tom Banner, Mr. and Mrs. Sidney Banner, Mr. and Mrs. David Banner (brothers and sisters-in-law); Mr. Ernest G. Hughes (brother- in-law), Mrs. Walter Dennett (sister-in-law), and Mrs. McKenzie (aunt). Many beautiful floral tributes were sent.

A GOLDEN WEDDING CHAT.

BV AN "EXAMINER "‘ REPRESENTATIVE.

. The above interesting announcement, which tiPieured in the "Manchester Guardian" on Monday morning, told its own, tale* and a secret, which had been well kept, having become public property, friends and admirers in all parts of the country hurrAed up with their congratulations and their toke Tis of respect and affection, born of a service that must leave its impress upon the life of the -country at many points Wnere Mr. Yonge and his devoted help- meet have laboured for half a century—and more. • • . . - • '

As I knew.. tile event was to be celebrated quietly, I did not intrude upon the privacy of tue revered couple on Monuay, llpetillSe oi the fact that I should naturally appear. before them, not only as a bearer of all good, wishes, but as a..proiessiunal man in quest. of copy: .1 made my call on Tuesday afternoon, anu iound quite a delightful air about the house, 26, Ellesmere- road, 6tockton Heath.

The furniture of. the house was literally covered with Ilowers—rare, sweet smelling blooms of many kinds, breathing of kindly thoughts, and demonstrating a desire to express them in the niost delicate, unostentatibus, Zrut 'yet, at the same time, effective, manner possible., iill Busy.

But perhaps the most natural state of things to lulu was that mr. lunge was 1101 at noine, !laving telt Vv arrtngton by an early LIIIIII to attenu a meeting at Ailistiaieui tiie Laneashtre Congregational union, of Willett Ile is Still secre- tary.

Airs. Yonge was , the personification of gra- iousness in ner receptiun.

" e celebrated the event yesterday,"' she said, if it may be called a celebration, quietly, in tne- company of few friends. I never had .so many !lowers in my house before, and the number of telegrams and letters that • arrived yesterday was remarkable. The neighbours, Who did not know what Was 'happening, grew alarmed- at the procession of telegraph boys that arrived, and at length, knowing that Mr. Yonge had gone out early that morning, kindly tent to inquire whether there had been an acci- dent.

A comment ,on Mr. Yonge's continued activity, following upon i his retirement, caused Mrs. Yonge to remark, with 'not a little evident pride, "Yes, our friends sometimes say he might as well have remained on active duty. Mr. Yonge, who is wonderfully well, is really very busy. He has preached for the last six Sun- days, and he will preach for the next six. Last Sunday he was away at Lancaster; next Sun- day (to-morrow) he will 'preach at Wycliffe. The County Union work also keeps him well occupied."

Mrs. Yonge.

I succeeded in getting Mrs. Yonge to say a little. about herself, though only a little. It is well-known, however, that she has been greatly interested in all the labours of her hus- band, and that she has always been ready to assist whenever help could be rendered. Dur- ing Mr. Yonge's long pastorate in Warrington, her visits to [he sick and distressed were very frequent, while the delicacies she was able to provide were always appreciated and did much good. For many years Mrs. Yong dispensed

the Wycliffe .Cheer Fund, and by its means gladdened the hearts and increased the com- forts of the less fortunate members of the Church.

While in a reminiscent mood', she told me that the chapel in which she Was married had been 'altered .-since' : into -arrinstituto. "I did a good deal of- work there," she , said, ," and my husband was at College. The institute is ,now a great centre of church work in London, and was recently .visited., by -the Queen,. who .Saw, everything, and heard the women-.and girls sing." Owing to the difficulties that could not be overcome, only one of the family was able to be present on Monday, though i the others sent messages that will be among those most highly prized.. . The eldest son, Mr. J. A. Yonge, M.A., J.P., is head master of the celebrated Silcoates Sctfool, Wakefield; Herbert, the second son, has been in Toronto seventeen years; and William, the youngest son, who was present, is in business in Manchester.

Mr. Yonge's Eloquence. As one who Tias,followed with a close appre- ciation the career of the. Rev. ..John Yong in Warrington;. and noted. especially the result of his labours at Wycliffe Church, and as one also who had the greateSt for.his silver; tongue, which raised him far above the average of the thousands of public spealiersl have met, lay or clerieal, I have pleasure in adding- my congratulations—in which I know Me thousands of " Examiner " readers will join--to Mr. Yonge and his good lady in the hope that; in the words of the Wycliffe P.S.A. resolution, they may h—tive many. years of happluess still' before them " with, peace at eventide." . T. W.

A WYCLIFFE REMEMBRANCER.

The Rev. John yenge was pastor of Wycliffe Congregational Church for, 36 years, and re- Wed about twelve months ago, amid universal regret. At last -Sunday's service at the church-7°n the eve of the anniversary—the following letre7; to Mr. and Mrs. "Yonge was submitted to an approving congregation, and duly forwarded : "Wycliffe Congregational Church, Warrington,' - 24th August, 1913. Dear Mr. and Mrs. Yonge,— . We, the members of the: church and con--. gregation at Wycliffe, are aware that on Monday, August 25th, you will 'celebrate the fiftieth anniversary of your wedding day, and we desire to offer you our hearty congratulations and sincere good wishes on this glad occasion. Of the sincerity of our good-will it must be unnecessary to assure you, for it grows out of the many years of loving and united s4rvice which you have rendered to us, the memory and inspiration of which will long endure. It is our hope and prayer that you may'both be granted yet a long period in which to enjoy the happy companionship you have known through so many year's, and that this special day, laden as it must be with many.recollections of the past,. may be to you a day of great blessing and de- light. • Many whom you have endeared to you by loyal friendship and true kindliness of heart will be thinking of you at this time of rejoicing, and we wish very affec- tionately to assure you of our sincere prayer that the presence of GA may be with you in full measure, and that He may crown the future with supreme gifts of happiness and peace. On behalf of the church and congregation, • Yours very affectionately, T. T. JAMES, Minister; Z. AamivAGE, J. BILL, W. BESWICK, W. BROWN, C. ECCLES B. FARRAR, E. S. GARLAND; J. HAWTHORN. E. WALLINGTON,' P. T WINSKILL, Deacons.'..' •

NO REASON AGAINST IT.

I am serious when I say we are quite pre- pared to argue this question of self-government for the people of Ireland with our opponents. "The Altogetherness of' Everythinir'

in Nature.

SEA AND LAND.

One has not become an intelligent observer of Nature for loniA before realising the wonder- ful unity and harmony of all her ways, and the marvellous manner in which all the varied, and apparently warring, parts work together for the comnion good of the Whole. One is im- pressed, as the old German- philosopher Hegel was, with. the " altogetherneSs of everything." One cannot study one aspect. of natural history without in the process beingbrought into im- mediate touch with many other aspects. For instance, the side issue raised. by the editorial note in last week's article on tbiz," overlapping" of the countryside and the seaside is full of sug- gestive and interesting points. That the countryside and seaside—or, to reduce them to ,simpler terms—the land, and water, are inti- ,mately related is an obvious fact. After all, what we call the sea is only land hidden from view by great bodies of salt water. Could we with a. gigantic broom of isome Mrs. Partington sweep back for a short time all the water under a hundred fathoms deep round the British Isles, we should find that the sea-bed has its moun- tains and valleys, and even its great fresh-water springs--some of them giving forth over ss million gallbris a day—just like the dry land. We should also find that the British Isles occupy air elevated position on the sea-bed, which forma a. continuous shelf round the Euro- pean Coast, with the exception of two or three places, such as part of the channel between Scotland and Ireland, and the entrance to Loch Fyne—famous for its herring—the waters round our islands tare fess than a hundred fathoms deep. Or, to put the facts the other way round, if the:sea bottom were raised six hundred feet all the waters round these islands would disappear. Much, very much, of the sea round our shores is less than a hundred fathoms deep. Oh the Lancashire and Cheshire shore we should have to go ten miles from land before reaching even the ten fathoms line, and off the rocky coast of North Wales the water is only twenty-five fathoms deep. On the east coast we find the depth of the North Sea from Lin- colnshire to Holland is never, more than ten fathoms. The deepest part round the British Coast is off Galway, where the water goes sud- denly down to abyssal depths. Further north, north of the Shetlands, there is a high ridge of mountains on -the floor of the sea, extending right across to the Coast of Greenland. On each side of this ridge the sea dips down rapidly to depths of 1,750 fathoms. The sea-bed—the superficial layer, at least—differs very much according to-the nearness and character of the adjoining land. Near the land the bottom con- sists of boulders, gravel, sand, and materials brought down from the land by the rivers, and the wear and tear of coast erosion. The finer particles are carried in suspension in the water longer-and are taken further out to sea. The Influence of Climate.

This suggests another, aspect of the relation- ship of land and sea—the influence of climate upon them both. The destructive work done by frost, storms and rain on the land is enor- mous, and only. when one begins to examine the evidence does one become aware of the power of these forces. We read frequently in the public prints of huge masses—weighing thousands of ,tons—of chalk cliffs on the York- shire and Kent coasts breaking away and 'fall- ing into the sea. These falls usually happen after a period of severe frost, which causes great fissures to form. The same thing occurs on a smaller scale in the limestone districts in- land, as witness the great "Scars" on the River Wharfe in Yorkshire. Nearer home, on the banks of the Mersey between Hale and Garston, great portions of the cliffs of boulder clay are continually being broken away by the frosts. In the various mountain ranges of Britain the crags arc, broken away in Masses by the same agency. No rocks are too hard for the expand- ing and cOntracting power of King Frost. Frost, rain, storms and wind are gradually levelling our mountains and hills, and trans- ferring them to the sea. Every student of geology knows the action of these forces on the land. Every sharp frost loosens great quan- tities orrock from the mountain side, and every shower of rain washes the sand and soil into the mountain streams, and thus, little by little, they reach the rivers, where the stones are ground into gravel and transferred nearer and nearer the sea with every " spatt " or flood. Thus the land is gradually, slowly, but stnrely 'being denudated. Sometimes after a great storm the destructive power of the storms is forced on our attention by some damage done near at hand, but the gentle rain showers, of Which few take notice, are proportionately, 4hough quietly, working in the same way. it would be interesting, were the figures available, to know how much some of our high mountains have lost in height during the last century, owing to the crumbling and grinding agencies of Nature. Likewise, were it possible to ob- tain the information, it would be equally inter- esting to learn how many hundred tons of the solid substances—the bricks and mortar„the red sandstone of the buildings, the paving stones of the side-walks and streets—of War- rington were annually crumbled to sand and dust by frost and acid laden atmosphere, then washed away by rain, and transferred via Mit gutters and drains to the sea. Last Year's Rains. .

The excessive rains of last year played ' great havoc AN over the three kingdoms, In some districts making distinctive changes on the landscape. During the present -month we saw many instances of this. A day spent at\ Ardentinny, on the Shores of Loch Long, re- vealed many traces of the great damage done by the great cloud burst in November last. We climbed one of the mountain sides arid found that great ravines had been cut into the rock, which were of the Silurnian grit formation. One in particular was over thirty feet deep. Acres of trees had been torn up by the root and washed down the hill, and great masses of rock weighing many tons had been hurled down the mountain, tearing up the trees in their pas- sage. At the bottom of the mountains five bridges were torn up and washed bodily Into the sea, and a long length of the road carried away. In addition to this, the road along the, lochside was blocked for miles by fallen trees. Nine months after—in August—contractors were still repairing the damage, which, to the road alone, was said to amount to £lO,OOO. Another instance of the power of rain we saw at Afrochar, at the head of Loch Long. In February last one of the largest landslides that have occurred within living memory took place. The whole side of a mountain ilid'down to the sea, with a deafening roar. It was esti- mated that over four hundred thousand tons of bogland, brown heath and shaggy wood were displaced, leaving the rock face as bare as the slates on -a church roof. The landslide was caused by the incessant rains of the summer and autumn being held in the heath and bogs until thp water-logged land became too heavy and burst the bonds that bound it to the rock underneath. These are but two instances of the physical effect of water, a little imagination will easily picture the result of the play of such forces on the land, year after year. generation after generation. and century after century. The land must steadily diminish in height and the sea-bottom become higher. Another aspect, the work of rivers and currents, must be left till next week.

G. A. DUNLOP.

THE COMING MISSIONARY EXHIBITION.

Churchmen will rejoice to hear of the Missionary Exhibition to be held in the Empire Hall, towards the end of next Novem- ber. This should indeed help to spread inter- est in, and to arouse a sense of responsibility for, religious work abroad. There are, how- ever, many men in Lancashire who hold what seem to them to be valid and reasonable objec- tions Co missionary work of any kind, and, accordingly, I venture to ask a question in your columns relating to the organisation of this local effort. Will this exhibition merely be a collection of curios, interestingly explained by earnest lecturers? Or will there be present those who from their cwn experience and know- ledge will be able to deal with the immense problems--problems of philosophy, of colour questions, of advancing scientific discoveries— Which face the Church abroad to-day? If so, at this exhibition, affecting as it will, a wide area In South-West Lancashire, there will be a great oPportunity so to present missionary work that sincere objectors will see our efforts in their proper light, and become convinced of the urgency and importance of religious labour in the mission field. Winmarleigh-street

I. S. TOWNROE

===== 0002601_19130830_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
THINGS MEDICAL, SANITARY, AND

HYGIENIC.

Sir,--The great congress of the world's medi- cal men has come and gone, and we have seen and heard a great deal about ttie past and pre- sent of medical science. The address on medicine and surgery by Pro- fessor Harvey Cushing lets in a Hood of light on the prospects and position of the medical treatment of the future. Truly, as the Pro- fessor says, "The practitioner of the future can- not be chemist, neurologist, bacteriologist, ophthalmologist, radiographer, surgeon, and what not, rolled into one, and do effective work; the Jonathan Hutchinsons of the profession are rare. As the co-operation of those. expert in special lines becomes more and more necessary, the tendency will grow for conjoint studies of individual cases of diseases to be carried out in Properly equipped hospitals, where the data essential for a diagnosis can be more quickly and effectively accumulated and the para- phernalia for treatment kept in smooth running order from constant use." In view of the recent development of National Health Insurance, would it not be wise to develop matters in such a way that all our hos- pitals should become medical colleges, or semin- aries, and that all our medical men might be affiliated thereto, that all medical benefit funds should be paid into its funds, and that it should deal with the distribution of such funds among the profession? I think such a system would enable medical men to specialise in certain branches, and give them an opportunity of be- coming pre-eminent in their special line; it would give the patient a far better service and prospect of a permanent cure, while the doctor would be more secure of payment. for his ser- vices and the State have better returns for its expenditure. Another point of equal importance to the cure of disease is its prevention. The hygienics and sanitation of our towns should be highly specialised under the absolute coittrol of the officer of health, his chief and subordinate inspectors, and not divided under two or three committees. A divided department is wiry in- effective. The responsibility for the cleanliness of a town should be in the control of one auth- ority—the Cleansing Committee. For street cleaning purposes the town should be divided UP into districts, a squad of men for each dis- trict, being continually kept In that district and responsible to the inspector in charge for its cleanliness. There might be a leading man, but there is no necessity for a foreman in costly uniform at a big salary. No person. excent the Officer of Health and his chief inspector, should be nermitted to ride a bicycle while superin- tending cleansing work, for he should be in a position not only to insnect the byways. but the nooks and corners of his district. If these sug- gestions were carried out, considerable benefit would accrue to the community at large. SANITAS.

SIR W. LEVER'S TOUCHING LETTER.—A letter was read at Lymm ,Council meeting on Thursday from Sir William Lever, in reply to the resolu- tion of sympathy sent from the Council at the last meeting about the death of Lady Lever. He wrote to the 'Clerk thus: Dear Mr. Mullard,—l am deeply touched and Comforted by the kindly expressions of sympathy you have so tenderly and sincerely conveyed to me on the loss of my dear wife, and life-long companion. She lived her life solely for those she loved, and has sur- rendered it without leaving behind her the memory of a single unkind thought, or word, or deed. The achievement of her life is sacred to all who knew her; and all the kind, unselfish acts she did for others are now twining foliage and sprigs of scented flowers around her memory. Words are all too feeble to express the loss we all suffer. There never was, nor is, nor can ever be, a more loving, gentle, true and kind, unsel- fish wife, mother,- sister, and friend."

A BIT or CONIrRING.-Lymm appears to be an unfortunate place for horses at present. It was witlr great difficulty that one of Messrs. E. Brown and Sons' horses was rescued from the Sow Brook on Tuesday; whilst there *as the tipping accident at the Delph on the 21st inst., a further development of which was enacted on Friday morning of last week, when Carter Davies went to the stables, where he found that the stall usually occupied by his horse was in the possession of a strange and noble steed. Apparently some kind friend of the Council had heard of the accident to one of the horses, and, with commendable forethought, had presented that public body with a fine specimen of the Equus Asinus genus. Inquiries as to the advent of the volunteer were without avail until later in the day, when the rightful owner put in an appearance to claim his quadruped, which had. escaped. The animal was relinquished with many regrets, having brought much mirth into the humdrum duties of the day.

CHOIR SUNDAY AT PRIMITIVE METHODIST CHURCH. —Choir Sunday was celebrated at the Lymm Primitive Methodist Church on Sunday. Mr. G. Watson, of Glasgow, preached in the morn- ing from the words, "Where there is no vision the people perish." ,The same gentleman also occupied the pulpit in the evening, when .he delivered an inspiring address from the text, I. Cor. x. 13. Taking " Temptation "as his dominant theme, the preacher showed how that (1) the presence of temptation indicated the pre- sence of God; (2) God'& faithfulness is tempta- tion's limit; (3) the way of escape is the way of God. At this service the ehoir gave a very fine rendering •of Sullivan's exacting anthem, "Hearken unto Me."—ln the afternoon the choir gave a sacred concert, the subject of which was "The dependence of man on the goodness and guidance of God." Miss M. J. Appleton considerably enhanced her reputation by her chaste and reverent singing of "None other lamb." Miss Bessie Cross is a promising vocal- ist, and in Haydn's setting of "0 Thou Who driest the mourner's tear" showed a marked advance, on her previous' efforts. "0 glorious land" was sung by Mr. H. Clare with all the spirit and fire demanded by; the song, though the singer was handicapped by a cold. Mr. George Appleton made his first solo appearance 'at these services, and both surprised and de- lighted his audience with his vocal abilities in "Thou didst leave Thy throne." A duet by Miss M. Clare and Mr. G. Appleton, and a quar- tette by the Misses M. J. Appleton and Dora Cross, and Messrs. 11. Clare and Arthur Apple- ton, were also well sang. The choral numbers comprised anthems from Mendelssohn, Breiss- mann, and others, and their rendering reflected credit both on the choir and the conductor, Mr. J. Edwards.; the palm would probably be awarded to "Cast thy burden on the Lord," with its captivating melody in the tenor, and the wealth of tender expression which the choir threw 'into the music. The skilful playing of Miss Shore contributed in no small degree to the success of the musical portion of all the ser- vices throughout the day. Good attendance characterised the afternoon, and evening meet- ings, hut the morning congregation was some- what disappointing.

PRETTY FLOWERS.

Workingmen's Fine Display at Broom Edge Show.

INTERESTING SPORTS.

Broom Edge was en fete on Saturday, smiles and gala dress were the order of the day, the occasion being the tenth annual show and sports of the Broom Edge ad District Horticul- tural Society.

It is pleasing to note that this society, the only one in the Lymm district, is carried on by a committee composed largely of working men, whose strenuous efforts to make the sli(k- a suc- cess this year was supported by the "Clerk of the Weather," who made ample amends for stormy conditions the previous day, and the fine weather produced a record attendance. Mr. E. Landless; the indefatigable secretary, was ably assisted by his fellow officera and com- mittee in carrying out the arrangements. The numerous exhibits were tastefully staged f e in or aa large marquee, giving every opportunity careful inspection. Messrs. Caldwell and Son, of Knutsford, had: a splendid herbaceous stand, Their exhibits of roseS and !ilium tigrinum splendens being especially noticeable. Another exhibit that was much admired was the gorgeous display of sweet peas shown by Mr. Frank Hindley, which were afterwards sold for the benefit of the society. In the flower sections, roses and sweet peas appeared to be both popular and perfect; whilst amongst the vegetables, potatoes, onions and carrots were all worth special attention. Though apples are rather scarce this year, those exhibited were particularly fine specimens. There was a num- ber of good bouquets of wild flowers exhibited by the school children. The onerous task of judging was left to such skilful authorities as Messrs. Bennett (Lymm). Reid (Agden). and Parker (Mere), who after- wards expressed their satisfaction with the ex- hibits, and appreciation of the efforts of the committee. Following the custom of past years the Chester Society for, Home Teaching of the Blind were allowed to have a stall in the nufrquee....

in zadd itio n to the show a series of sports for the children took place, and these were heartily appreciated, many close finishes being wit- nessed. Excitement was provided by a race for married women, which resulted in a win _ for Mrs. Moss, with Mrs. Copeland and Mrs. Ackerly as second and third respeCtively. A new feature this time was a tea tent, where choice refreshments were dispensed by Mes- dames Landless, W. Mclntyre, Farrar and Miss mcintyte. Variety was afforded by side shows and donkey rides, and in the evening public dancing took place to the strains Of the Lymm Subscription Band, conducted by Nagle, which had been in attendance during the after- noon. The following is a list of officers and committee: President, Mr. 0. G. Moseley; hon. treasurer. Mr. J. Thomason; hon. secretary, Mr. E. Landless; and Messrs. .Jos. Spruce (chairman). Jos. Brazendale, John. Brazendale, A. Beard, E. Copeland, W. Daniels, J. Farrar. F. Hindley, W. Mclntyre, H. Whitelegg and W. Burgess. The prize list was as follows:

A HARDY ANNUAL.

Will Burford-lase Re Lit With Gas?

There was a somewhat lively discussion at the monthly meeting of Lymm District Council on Thursday evening, over that hardy annual, the suggested lightinc, of Burford-lane. The chair was occupied by 'Councillor It. H. Glotobed, in the absence of the Chairman, Councillor S. Davies, and others present were Councillors G. L. Welford, J. H. Holt, W. G. Groves, T. H. Warburton, W. Gould, T. F. W. Dixon, and F. Lockyer, and the officials were Mr. W. Mallard, clerk; Mr. D. Mort, surveyor; Mr. W. L. Don- aldson, gas manager; Dr. De Jong, medical officer.

Councillor Gould raised the question of the lighting of Burford-lane by moving that the Council recognise the necessity of lighting the lane, and take immediate steps to bring about the lighting of thelane as soon as possible. We have been waiting too long for this, he said. The question has been raised again and again, only for it to be hung up. The inhabit- ants of Broom Edge think they are never going to get it done. Councillor Dixon seconded Mr. Gould's pro, posal. The Chairman: Your resolution is that it should be an instruction to the Gas Committee. Councillor Gould: No. It is that the Council recognise the necessity for lighting the lane, and that instructions be given to take immediate 'tops. Councillor Welford : We should not take such a step until we know where we are about the eost. This is the last possible step any intelli- gent body of men should take. I have not been a party to such a thing since I have come on the Council. I think the matter should be brought before the 'Gas Committee. Does Mr. Gould mean to light the whole of Burford-lane from Broom_ Edge to the Bridgewater Canal? Councillor Gould : Most certainly.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Gould Surprised.

Councillor Welford : This, scheme, then, is going to cost a big sum of money, and the ques- tion is: What does it mean to us in cost? The gas is costly, and it is not making a profit, and yet we are asked to go into this scheme without having a single figure as regards cost before us. This seems to be bordering on absolute blind- ness, especially in regard to the financial under- taking we have to administer. The first step is to bring the matter before the Gas Com- mittee. and I move that it be referred to the committee.

Councillor Gould: I am surprised at Mr. Wel- ford saying that. We have referred the matter to the Gas Committee several times since I have been elected tb this Council three years ago. We have not, and shall never get, satisfaction from the committee. If we now sent it to them it will be deferred for years. The cost will not be great, and I ask the Council to deal with it. Councillor Groves: I want to get the cost pre- pared for the next meeting of the Council.

Councillor Gould: We havo'had the cost once. Councillor Groves: We were not in the posi. 'ion to borrow money before, but we can under our new Act. Meantime the committee could get at the cost. Urgency Recognised.

Councillor Holt: I remember it being said in this Council that when we got our Bill passed, we would have powers to borrow money to carry out the lighting of Burford-lane. We all recog- nise the urgency of the matter, but I think we would be well advised to see first what the scheme is going to cost, and then exercise the powers we have got. Councillor Gould (warmly): Is it meant that this is to go on for ever?

The Chairman: I feel as strongly as Mr. Gould about this matter, but I think it is wiser to get at the cost before deciding anything. When it comes before the Gas Committee, I shall certainly eMpport the scheme. Councillor Groves: I move that the Gas Man: biter be empowered to get the cost for the next meeting of the Council.

ConnCillor Gould: My resolution is better. The Chairman thought the figures might be got out for the next meting.

"Give the Public Some Idea."

Councillor Gould: Yes, I agree with that. want the Council to give the public some idea when they are going .to get it done. Has any meniber here any objection to the lighting of Burford-lane? I don't think so, but the mere getting out of the figures will not do for me. We, as a Council, must recognise the necessity of lighting Burford-lane, and then we refer it to the committee to immediately take steps to get information, with a view of proceeding with the work.

Councillor Groves: I understand your resolu- tion was to give instructions to immediately do the work.

Finally Councillor Groves withdrew his sug- gested amendment. Councillor Welford's amendment, which was seconded by Councillor Lookyer, was put to the. meeting. There voted in favour of the Welford amendment: Messrs. Welford, Groves, Lockyer, Holt and Warbur- ton-5. For the motion, -Mr. Gould. The resolution was thus defeated by five to one, and the matter goes to the Gas Committee.

GRAPPENHALL.

ANNIVERSARY SERMONS.-.-The anniversary ser- mons in connection with the Independent afetho- dist Chapel, Chester-road, were delivered on Sunday, when two were preached by Mr. A. M. Heaton, of Stretford. In the afternoon the Rev. J. 8. Chedburn, of Latchford, delivered an ad- dress to the young people. Special hymns and the anthems, " t.) come, let us sing," and "Incline thine ear to me," were creditably sung by an augmented choir, under the leadership of ld.P. Gough. Mr. T. Leah, with his usual ability, presided at the organ. Collections were taken at the close of each service in aid of the church expenses. The needs of a new place of worship were well exemplified, judging by the large con- gregations. The collections totalled .28 36.- "

• DEITH Or A FARMER.-Mr. Thomas Percival, of Moss Side Farm, Moore, a well-known rearer of horses and cattle, died on Saturday. , He was treasurer of the local Shire Horse Society, and for many years exhibited at local shows. He was 69 when he died. The funeral took place at the Grappenhall Churchyard, the service being conducted by the Rev. L. T. Darwell, vicar of St. John's Church, Walton.—The mourners were: Messrs. Joseph, Arthur and Thomas Per- cival (sons), Philip Darbyshire (son-in-law), Jameq, Charles. Daniel and John Percival (brothers), John Whitlow, Thomas Baxter, tieorge kinsey, John Longshaw and Eyes (brothers-in-law), Mr. E. Aubrey White (solici- tor), Dr. Smith, Mr. L. Bennett (Warrington and District Farmers' Auction Co., Ltd.).

Strange Early Morning Episode.

The inhabitants of Thelwall-view, at the corner of Bellhouse-lane, will not readily for- get the exciting episode which occurred on Tues- day morning. About four o'clock Mr. S. H. Jenkins was aroused by a loud knocking at the front door. He got out of bed to ascertain, the cause, and was encountered/ by a respectably dressed man, armed with a wooden post, about four feet in length, with which he had knocked in one of the panels of the door. At the next door he dropped two watches through the letter- box, presumably to attract attention. Mr. Jenkins drove the man away with the aid of a stick p.nd went to inform the police, but by this tithe the man had gone away. P.C. Jones was soon in pursuit, being joined later by Inspector Brease, and they succeeded in capturing their man about nine o'clock and took him to the Stockton Heath Police Station, when it waslhought that the service's of a medi- cal man had better be requisitioned, as he made all sorts of rambling statements. Dr. Hutton was called in and pronounced the man to be insane, and ordered his removal to Chester Asylum.

FRODSHAM.

CAUSING MIDNIGHT DISTURDANCES.—SGperintOI2- dent Enniozi, at Runcorn Police Court, on Mon- day, alleged that Frodsham youths visited Run- corn on Saturday nights to create disturbances with Runcorn youths. He said that in conse- quence of a lot of young fellows coming from Frodsham on Saturday evening and, quarrelling with local youths in Lowland-road' about mid- night, there had been numerous complaints to tho police. A young man named Walter Wat- son, of Prince's-street, was engaged in an argu- ment with other youths on Saturday when the police arrived, and he was arrested for being drunk and disorderly. A fine of 10s. and costs, was imposed. •

STOCKTON HEATH

TOWNSHIP TITTLE-T

There were Some joyful young hearts in the district this week.

These belonged to the clever popils Stockton Lodge School. who figured ;is success- ful in. the Oxintd Local ExaMinations.

The school, considering its siza. (lid as \veil as any school in the whole of the area covered hy the examinations. •

Sweet peas are the. pride of some of the " Heathens' " gardens. All report that this season has proved an excellent one.

Eight individual flowers upon one stalk was found in one garden. Can Latchford equal that? -

" Did you ever try to work?" queried a lady of the Heath of a stalwart tramp begging alms. " Missus, it's like this. Doctors says we should eat before we work. When I eat and try to work it gives me indigestion terrible, so, rather than ruin me constitution, I've been com- pelled to give up work."

RECRADITE PRESENTATION. -At the monthly meeting of the Sunbeam Tent, Independent Order of Rechabites, held at the Wesleyan Schoolroom on Monday, an interesting event took place. A presentation was made by Bro. H. Brereton (on behalf of the Juvenile Tent and a few adult members) to Miss J. Ellison, of Orford-avenue, of a silver-plated combination cruet (supplied by Bro. S. Fernley, Bridge. street), as a slight recognition of the esteem in which she is held at Stockton Heath Rechabite Tent, and to wish her long life and happiness on her approaching marriage. Miss Ellison, in a few .well-chosen words, returned thanks to the members for their kindness.

/ OBSTRUCTING A TRAM.

Stockton Heath Car Held Up.

For delaying Stockton. Heath bound car seven minutes, Joseph Whitlow, 4, Clegg-street, Warrington was ordered to pay 305., including costs, at Warrington Police Court on Thursday. It was stated this caused inconvenience to Stock- ton Heath passengers, and it was alleged that Whitlow was driving a float and got on to the tram metals at Wilderspool-causeway, near the Causeway Hotel, and kept persistently upon them until the tram terminus at Stockton Heath.

Patrick Higgins said he was driving the 10.50 a.m. tram to Stockton Heath, and defendant got on the track. He rang the gong repeatedly, but defendant would not move off the line and kept ahead of the tram. He was seven minutes late at Stockton Heath.

Whitlow: I was doing above 12 miles an hour and you never asked me to get out of the way. You know as much about it as my hat. John Burrows, conductor, corroborated Driver Higgins, and Henry Clough, the next witness, said he was a painter. He was working at Wilderspool Brewery and his attention was called to the incident by the continuous ringing, of the tram gong. "I do not know whether the driver of the car was cursing, but he was shout- ing plenty,'? added witness, amidst laughter. Mrs. Lucy Fleming, 2, Heath-street,. Stockton Heath, said she was a passenger on the tram to Stockton Heath. She heard the gong continu- ally ring and the ear could not go faster than a walking pace owing to defendant's float being on the line. She had to complain ahout the delay caused. Sergeant Reddington said he interviewed Whitlow, and he said that he did not know the car was behind until he got to the swing bridge at Stockton Heath. Superintendent Williamson: He did not say he was deaf F—No.

Whitlow said his float was making a noise, such as Ile could hear nothing.. That, com- bined with a spirited horse to Watch, prevented him from knowing the ear was behind. Had he heard the gong Ile would have got out of the way. Driver Higgins, recalled, said that the noise of a float would not drown the sound of the tram gong. It was impossible to drive the float at ten or twelve miles an. hour. .

SANKEY GAS.

Warrington to be Asked to Make

Concessions.

THE NEW BRIDGE.

It is the intention of the Sankey Parish Council to approach the 'Warrington Corpora- tion with a view of obtaining some concessions in the price of gas tO consumers in the village. The :natter canie up • for discussion at the monthly meeting of the Parish Council on Tuesday evening, „Nytien a letter was read- by the Clerk (Mr. J. Smith) from Mr: Long, one of the members of the Parish Council, who urged the necessity for the erection of a lamp be- tween Penketh Lane Ends and Mr. lielsall's farm. He pointed out that in view of the prospective . reduction in the price of gas of one penny per thousand feet by the Warrington Corporation, the parish of Great • Sankey was entitled to further concessions.

The Clerk said that the erection of new pro- perty on the road in question had caused a demand for better lighting. The lamps, how- ever, cost 50s. each per year to maintain.

Mr. S. Fair: It. is not much for the safety of the public. Mr. W. Furness said he would like to point out that during the last five or six years it had always been the desire of the Parish Council as far as possible, not to increase 'the cost for gas lighting. They had been successful in recent years in extending their gas supply without encroaching upon any extra charge on the older rate for that purpose. He thought the time had come when they might approach the Warrington Corporation Gas Com- mittee, with a view of getting another conces- sion. Presuming they got such a concession. any advantage would be used for the purpose of extending their lighting supply. They had no desire to increase 'the cost of lighting in the parish. In view of the fact that the Warring- ton Council had made a reduction to their con- sumers, Great SankeY. as an increasingly large customer, ought to have a concession that would enable them to provide one or two additional. lamps.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, AUGUST 30, 1913,

THE WORLD OF LETTERS.

New Books.

A Prisoner in Fairyland.

- - , Alernon Blackwood. London: Macmillan and Co., Ltd.

Mr. Blackwood's new story, "A Prisoner in . Fairyland," centres -round the idea which at the present time is demanding so much con- sideration from serious-minded people, namely, the tremendous influence of Thought, an influ- ence -thateaeli:of us can wield for the good, not only of those around us, but of the, world at large. The book should touch the hearts Of all those who, like Henry Rogers, the hero Of the tale. have sympathy with sorrow and suffering, and can enter into the troubles and joys of others. Even as a boy there had burned in him the desire 'to give himself for , others, to help afflicted folk, to make the world go -round a little more easily." But to bring his dreams to accomplishment he must have. means.. So, to all appearanms, he becomes little more than the keen man of busineSs, till at forty he finds hithself possessed of :sufficient: capital to retire from the daily grind of office work and embark upon the Various philanthropic schemes he had never ceased to cherish against the time when he could realise them. But first he resolves to take a holiday, hnd on a spring day pays a visit to the home of his boyhood in a little Kentish village. There, as , he wanders alone in the garden, fairy beliefs , of long ago re-assert themselves in bewildering array. He sees again the ,Starlight Express, a' fairy train which, to his childish fancv, carried passengers to ihe Star Cave, where lost star- light was stored for sprites to gather and -cast broadcast over the world. Yet this quaint idea that, took possession of him seemed as in former times not his own imagination, but " put into him by.sonieone else."

To. this short holiday succeeds a longer one. Rogers goes to Bourcelles,.a village in the Zura Mountains, where he had stayed live-and- lAventy years ago, and where a cousin is now living. This cousin is an-unsuccessful- author: who is struggling along to the best of his ability with a somewhat incompetent wife and family of delightful children. Into their midst and indeed into the whole place, Rogers brings an atmosphere that turns the little village into "the fairyland of sympathy, of sweet endeavour, love, gentleness and -sacrifice for others." We see him, in fact, and others with him, in two lives, the life he lived by. day, and also in what Mr. Blackwood describes as "the vaster sea of life ^we lead below the surface when we sleep." For by night' Rogers slips -away from his sleep- ing body. and beautiful journeys are made to the star Cave, where he gathers the starlight that when shed upon others. does so much to brighten the sadness of their lives and turn gloom into, radiance. In the end liogers's own heart's desire is fulfilled, for, by a singular co- incidence, he learns into „WhOSC network of Beautiful Thoughts he has been caught. and held ever since he was a boy, and that the two Should become one is inevitable. From begin- ning to end the book is intensely interesting, and if we read with understanding we cannot but be the better for the deep yet situple spiritual teaching of this beautiful _story.

Plant Life.

By Prof. J. B. Farmer, D.Sc., Ens. London: Williams and Norgale. (Home University Library), is. and 2.5. 6d.

_ . One of the earlier issues of the Home Univer- sity Library ccuitained a volume on 'Y The Evo- lutiOn. of Plants," by Dr. D. H. .Scott, which was practically limited to an examina- tion Of the fossil reoorkts. The Professor of Botany in the Imperial College of Science now contributes a volume explaining some of the salient features of living plant forms from the point of view of function; and the causes Of "the marvellous beauty and adaptedness of form which are so apparent in every branch of the_ vegetable kingdom."

TO have atteMpted.to.cover the_whole ground U) SO small a space would have been to produce difir cram-book,. atid4here are too many of that kind already. Instead,; • Professor Farmer hag 'wisely chosen to deal more fuHy with a few instances of structure and adaptation in relation to the two great functions of feeding and reproduction, and so to impress the reader once with the difficulties and the immense interest of his subject. There is no pretence of conveying pontifically a body of dogmatic 1:11(11V- ledge. Professor Farmer .constantly tells his render plainly that we do not know" this or that. Not only can he afford to do so, for the hook Is full of evidence of long original re- search ; but we have all the stimulus of feeling ourselves on the borderland.. where intelligent work may at any moment win the reward of a great discovery.

The twenty-eight illustrations deserve a specH word of praise.

Literary Gleanings.

FIRING TUE GUN.

The click and silence that followed the wora " Fire !" were quite eloquent enough to me of all the shattering damnation they represented-L, a projectile weighing eight hundred and fifty Pounds hurtling .to its mark at the rate of al- most a thousand y'ards a second. But we in the turret would .have known nothing ; for before it had reached the target the breech-block would have opened to the screech of the air blast which cleans out the burning fragments of -cordite in the breech and afterwards the ramnter would have shot in with Its mop and out again; the ammunition-hoist would have come clattering and .screaming up, another pro- jectile would 'have relied into the tray with an- other two hundred-Weight of death packed behind it, the rammer would .have pushed it home with a kick, the block would have swung again, the great gun would have been sighted_ and swung in the air, again the word would, have been given, and again the fragment of ctmcentrated power that men had toiled in faCtories and drawing offices. in laboratories and- foundries to 'perfect, would have .been sent winging through the sea air to spend itself in destruction.--From " With the Fleet," by Filson Young ((rant ffichards, publisher.)

THE JUMP OUT OF BED.

When a man is in perfect health he wakes up naturally when he has had enough sleep. What is enough sleep is.entirely a matter of habit and Of individuality. One man may have had mugit when he has slept four hours. It is not woll for a man who is in the habit of sleeping right Or nine hours to cut down his night's rest anktcnly to four or five. The healthy .man wakes up ready to get up, ready to leave his bed. This is because he is too full of vigour to lie idle.. 'the. Mau in perfect health must he aotdve. But his awakening need uot necessarily he sudden. Many men wake up gradually. • Such an awakening is pleasant. And is often accompanied by the greatest mental activity of the day. Poets have found themselves com- posing their most beautiful of verses, musicians imagining the loveliest of melodies when in that r,oidition of perfect physical rest., with the mind refreshed by a night of sleep. It is not so much the amount of sleep as its quality that counts. An Edison can get as much sleep in four hours as most of us get in eight, which means merely that his sleep is so intense, his rest so perreGt-;' Unit iii four hours all the fatigue poisons are driven from his system. while most men's sleep is so fitful or so light that it takes eight or nine hours to do the 5alllP work, for them. . It does not matter what time you go to bed long as you have ut regular hour and stick to it. The old saying that an hour of sleep holore midnight is worth two after it is ,not true. but it has this nitwit of truth in it ; that the earls- hours of sleep are worth more than the later. The aiw-rwil.:;rii i 5 hC.hg:c.;tC ed about

saine hour every night, anti it nialie !fitly difference whether this .hour be nine p.m. or three a.m. •

A CREE INDIAN ENCAMPMENT.

Recently, when returning from a fishing ex- pedition to White Bear Lake with a friend, we passed a Cree Indian encampment among the scenery that seemed to carry us home to the Sussex Downs. The Crees,- according to linguistic classification, are Algonquoins, and their original home was by Hudson's Bay ;.but ' they are now found throughout Saskatrllewan and Manitoba, I believe, on the," reserves" established or them. by the Government. In appearance they are decidedly Mongolian, this' peculiarity being in sonic case's Much inore noticeable than in others. Two men I saw had oblique. eyes, but high cheekbones, while more or less flat-looking, beardless faces and lank black hair were com- mon to almost all. The stoicism of the Indians has become a byword, and they certainly eliminate expression from their faces in the presence of a stranger. My companion; Mr. Le Mcsurier, had traded with them in the past. and lived among them for years, and, being well r,cquainted with their language, was looked upon as a friend, so that when he called out. the blank looks my presence had caused quickly gave place to an animation nearly equal to that of a European.—ll. IL Pittman, in "Country Life."

===== 0002601_19130830_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW ANIMALS CAME TO FLA.

An exhibition which is arousing considerable interest and attracting a large number of visi- tors to the Natural History Museum was .opened hi the public on August 15th. The exhibition, which has been in preparation for many months illustrates the modification of the struc- hire by which animals have attained command of the-,air. At the present time, When the science of aeroplaning is making such rapid advances, and when so Much attention is bring given by inventors to the construction of mach- inery adapted to the requirements of aviation, on exhibition of this character is peculiarly in 11w -fitness of things at our great national Museum of Natural History, and the authorities have been well advised in organising it. In the series of specimens exhibited, the adap- tation of each kind of flying animal for aerial loeomotien is explained, and the changes that must have taken place in the structure of the body before • the .animal could really fly are indicated. In this connection it is a refnait- able fact that the power of flight has been evolved independently in different groups of animals, e.g., bats, birds, pterodactyles and insects.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
JAPANESE GARDENS.

Gardens in Japan, whether of vast area or tiny things within the limits of a china tray. are all landscape gardens ; for they are designed to represent the scenery of the country in ideal forms. In • order to accomplish this the designer must be well acquainted with the. beautiful scenes of his native land, though he shall not serVilely copy any one of them: It is the same with the. Japanese ariist—he must observe birds or fish or trees in' such numbers that he is able to give you a picture not of any individual one, but rather of the ideal type— the essential features oi the whole species. Of course, it is also necessary to study min- utely the famous gardens that exist already : and in these he finds models of Such excellence that his natural artistic sense soou 'reaches a high level. For the ordinary journeyman .;ar• dener as he works. his way through the eountry sees the results of an art that has occupied the atlentiOn of sages, poets, and garden-hovers for generations; and seems-instinctively to ,grasp the motive and eesthetic principles involved ; thus the Japanese garden at its best may be fitly termed "-a modulation from pure nature to pure art.- There is nothing UP-hazard about the construction of it. The gardener, after care- fully considering the location, with its bearing on the house and the materials avilabtc knows beforehand just how the garden Will appear, for he sees it in his 'mind's eye; and knows just the place for every stone and every tree. The stones are placed first, as they forM the skeleton of the garden. They mak be large blocks of limestone of irregular shape, or peaks and outcroppings of rocks made by .joining together smaller stones with beautiful effect. • ,

The writer has often 'Watched the placing -of these stones—some of them' of immense size— and. seen the pains taken by the gardener and his associates in their .faultleSs arrangement. They will observe it from every point of view— suggestions made,,by even the humblest'of the party being duly considered—until the consen- sus of opinion pronounces it right. Simple though the operation of placing a stone may appear, an ideal is sought, and , when they finally bring it into being, the collective con- sciousness responds to the truth of it. With all the other features of the garden it 11s the same; for the gardener is an artist. and a master of his honourable craft. • He works unhur- riedly ; he will pause now and Then and take a few whiffs at his diminutive pipe as he contem- plates a piece of finished work. But he has got -something in the interval; and as he rc sumes his work there is none of the listless reluctance that comes of merely mechanical toil.

They say in Japan that gardeners live kilo, and no doubt there is a good reason for it. I spoke recently to OM of the old sehool nearly 80 years of age, but alert and full of buoyant. cheerfulness and good-will to planls.as well as to men. He was cultivating roses along witn the common nursery pints of Japan. and from the feeling way in which .he spoke of them I did not. wonder- at the richness :and profusion of their- blossoms.—E. Stephenson in "The Theosophical Path.",

THE SCEPTICISM OF POSTERITY.

Mr. Hilaire Be Hoc on "The Present in the Eyes of the Future."

"There is one very large, very sad and very certain truth," writes Mr: Hilaire Bettor in the September issue of "The Pall Mall Magazine," " about all the r6lations of past and future. That truth is this: the future will not believe us. It will not believe our most solemn and profound assertions. It will rationalise them, or ridicule them ; in one way or another it will explain them away, for that is the most certain thing about the attitude of men towards their remote, ancestors. They will believe the testimony of material things or of their own conjectures, but never the sworn word of their fathers. Were It not so there would be no room for historical crit'cism, or perhaps for history as a science at all."
