THE .-11 ALLS OF CHESTER.

Early Days and Scenes of an Old and Historic City.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME TURNS OF THE KALEIDESCOPE.

An appeal has just gone forth from the Dean and Chapter of Chester Cathe- dral on behalf of a fund for the restora- tion of the old Refectory of the Monks of .Chester. .Here in semi mm its walls grey with ugly whitewash. and cut up beyond recognition by modern walls,- is one of the finest, if not the very finest, monastic Refectories which Eng- land can show.

The old building, hidden under many shameful marks of neglect, is typical of much that lies quite close to us. It is one of those bits Of fossil history that becomes most interesting when placed against its true background. Chester has indeed so many anti- quities that the average visitor is con- fused,_ and some informed and friendly guide is needed, first to disentangle them and then to explain. Perhaps we can make Chester a little more interesting to the pilgrim by an effort to apply to it the historic kaleidescope.

their anxieties were unavailing, and afkr the slaughter Was complete upon the plain the savage Northmen turned upon the monks and ruthlessly destroyed them all. The possession of Chester .by Ethel- frith was of short duration. . It fell next 16 the Nfercians, who wrenched it from his grasp. Then came the day of the Anglo-Saxon, and a new era dawned. In this national upheaval Chester had also a part, for the city was seized by the Danes as they retreated westward. and here, behind the old walls, they .made a firm stand.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRST A ROMAN CAMP.

Chester came into .view 'at the dawn of the Christian era. The . Romans, advancing 'from the 'South in A.D. 47, and, led. by Ostorius Scapula, lighted upon the site of Chester and established there a military camp. From the name of the river they called it Deva, but later usage clung to Castra, or camp, and Castra, or -Chester, it has remained ever since. '

It would require no special military vision to discover the natural strength of the site of .Chester. On. two ‘sides it was . protected by the 'River -Dee; the River Mersey made the defence almost complete by flowing to join the Dee, across what is now the race 'course. A gentle hill of sandstone rock, sloping south and west to the Dee, and north to the Mersey, it was good either for de- fence or offence against the Welsh upon the opposite banks. There may, of course, have been a British town here before the Romans appeared. If so, all trace of it is lost. The first picture of Chester which can be safely drawn is that of a firmly made and eflicidnt camp. It has none of the ordinary interests or life of an.ordinary town. It IS a garrison .pure and, simple Here reside no fewer than 5,000 heavily armed men, who have seen service in Spain, Syria, Germany and Prance, and who now look with firm faces across the Dee in Prospect of fierce fighting with the Welsh. . These are the men of the 20th Legion, which bears as its other title 'Valeria.. Victrix. They build aroun,i them a stone wall, and Chester becomes a walled city. •

===== 0002601_19130830_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ROMAN TOWN.

Not only ,in the encircling walls, but in many other thipg4, these grim war- riors have left enduring marks, which we May study to the present hour.

.. The four main streets still remain as the Romans planned them. What is now called The Cross, in the centre of the city, was the Pretorium, and those who stand there and look down the main streets towards the four gate- ways in the ancient walls, are looking along the highways thaj, Were planned and .inhabited by the veterans of Rome. Scraps of the .Pretorium, traces that suggest a soldiers' hoSpital, soldiers' quarters, and a fine bath, have come to ;the surface to supply glimpses of that early day. The Roman Wall can be safely traced from Morgan's Mount to Newgate, and there are inscribed altars and memorial stones.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
A ROMAN BATH.

Most interesting is a Roman Bath which may be visited beneath one of the shops in Bridge-street, parts of which are placed 'upon view beneath the Water • Tower outside the City Walls. These • short stone pillars supported a Stone floor. ; Fires placed underneath produced the heat and gave what in modern times would be described as a Turkish bath., In Bridge-street can be seen the appara- tus for heating the chamber and the large water cistern in which the _bathers cooled and laved. • A similar bath was found near the Water Gate, and with it a tesselated pavement and an altar dedicated to Esculapius. These domestic things bring us much nearer in imagination and feeling to the Roman conquerors than anything else, and We advise visi- tors to see them. There are also in the Museum an altar to Jupiter and one erected by ,Flavius Longus, tribune of the xxth Legion, in honour of the Emperors Vespasian and Constantius.

For three hundred and fifty years the Romans held the. little city secure. From its gateways the legions marched with 'varying successes to fight the Welsh. It saw Paulinus begin his dis- appointing expedition against the Druids of Anglesey in A.D. 61, and 'six- teen. years later was the centre from which Agricola made fierce and suc- cessful onslaught upon North Wales.

Until about 410 A.D. Chester was a " castra " indeed. Then came the Roman retreat. It heard the clank of Roman armour for the last time as the 20th Legion marched forth, and silence fell upon its streets and walls.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0163.txt | included=true | included =====
BRITON AND SAXON.

The silence lasted for at least two centuries. With the lifting of the veil there comes a grim and aVful stor of the year 607. It is the tale of Ethel- frith, King of Bernicia, who led a Saxon force from the North, and sought to wrest this piece of territory from the Britons. A fierce battle took place beneath the Walls of Chester, and the Northman was victorious. Not far away was the Monastery of Bangor, arid it is said that 1,200 monks jour- neyed thence to the place of battle. They stationed themselves upon a hill to watch, and joined in united prayer for the overthrow of the invaders.. But

===== 0002601_19130830_art0164.txt | included=true | included =====
ALFRED AND THE DANES.

King Alfred, who knew the couNry -well, felt no difficulty at the situatiOn. He seized all the cattle in the neighbour- hood and destroyed or removed all the crops. There was no need for war- fare, for hunger was .more drastic .than the sword. The Danes :had marched into a mere prison in entering Chester and were glad to fly, leaving the town in ruins as they went. For seventeen years it stood neglected. .Then in 907 .came Ethelfreda, the d&ighter of. King Alfred and Wife of Ethelred of Mercia. This famous fighting lady repaired the walls of the city, extending them so as to take iii the small hill, on which the castle after- wards stood. CHESTER AS A SEAPORT.

Next in the line of changing scenes rises the picture of Chester as a seaport and a station of England's infant Navy. With the image of Chester pleasure boats before the mind, it is difficult to think of the Dee as having had any place in our commercial history. Yet a history of the Dee would be, up to a Certain point., a history Of English ship- ping. First the British coracle,, the Roman galley and Danish long boat; next the trading ships of Plantagenet and Tudor days, and the vessels that ply to the Irish Coast. Indeed it is only the silting up of the estuary that closes the chapter and leaves the River Dee to the toy boats and pleasure steamers.

And in the midst of the story, at the point we have reached, :we find Chester as a station of the tiny Navy, ivhich had grown under the fostering care of Edgar. Thus 'honoured, • Chester rapidly .became an important commer- cial centre. A steady 'flow of traffic. especially to and from Ireland. passed beneath its walls, and it became the undisputed port of the North-West. THE LAST SAXON STRONGHOLD.

It was, -perhaps. fitting. that Chester should be the last, .city in England to' hold out for the Saxon King Harold against the Conqueror... Saxon • hands had rescued it from ruin, and made it a fair and flourishing abode. Hera the Saxons made their last stand against William of Normandy. Changes came with the new Norman era, which cannot be fully told in a chapter so brief as this. gave the Earlship of. Chester to the famous Hugh Lupus, making Cheshire , a County Palatine, with power to hold its own Parliament and set up local Tribunals. -

CHESTER AS A NORMAN CAPITAL.

Hugh Lupus used his -powers to the, full. Eight baronk were appointed. who each shared with him the power of life and death over their subjects. Among these Cheshire was divided, and so began a government of the county, which lasted until the end of the sixteenth century. As late as 1597, it may be mentioned, the Baron of Kin- dertort was passing death sentences in his own home.. For live centuries Chester was the capital of the Earls of Chester.. The county was shut. out in.a large measure from the life of the nation. It Sent. no representative to Parliament. and lived a life and made a domestic histor of its own. • THE KALEIDESCOPE OF HISTORY.

Possibly this may be as much as the reader will desire to read of the ancient story of Chester: By its aid. he may seek: Out Some portion of the Roman wall ,and picture tire scenes iii which its cold stones have hao a Share. In his picture he will see • Briton, Roman, Dane and Saxon, and the sad welter. that preceded the coming of the Nor- than.

. . If the visitor carps ti turn the lialeidescope, further, he may readily do so. -He may See Archbishop saldwin preaching the Crusade; or the rallying of armies under Henry 11., King John, Henry 111., and Edward 1., in their campaigns,4gainst the restless ielsh. Again, he may see it held dur- g the Barons War for Simon de Mont- fort, or hear the shouts of mockery when Richard 11. is dethroned and carried hence to London. The attacks of the Welsh in the, struggle between the two intim-is are beyond all count- ing, and, the memory of pillage and ruin .weighs heavily. even yet. -Again the gates gates are armed and swords clash :! Chester becomes a fighting centre in us support of Nlargaret the Lancastr!an Queen.

- Yet again the Old walls are garri- soned in the Civil War, and Charles Stuart from one Of the towers watches the defeat of his army upon Rowton Moor. One wonders whether there is any- thing that has vitally louched. r,ngPirol that has not also vitally touched the little city On the Dee.

Ours is a " rough island story.- in- pd. and few places have had a more lefinite share in it than the calm old place' that now allures us, by quaint shops and pleasure skiffs, and that seems quite content, seeing that it has lived on into a quieter age to make no boast about its warlike past.

PISA'S RIVAL

Pisa..isTnot.-the only place to .possess a lean- h tower. There is also ix little-known one at Etampes.whictictates back to the sixteenth cen- tury. Recently it was thought that this tower was leaning too/much, and an expert investi- gation was ordered. The experts have conic to the 'conclusion that file position of the tower has not altered by a millimetre during the past three mnturics. Cardinal Richelieu had the tower examined in 1639, and a comparison of the figures then arrived at with those of the present experts shows no difference that can be measured by scientific instruments. .

The Little Bluebell.

One day in the clays when fairies played' about.. in their .lhossy dells there grew a little flower. Its colour was light blue, and it was shaped like a bell:. One day a fairy said to the flower, "I have come to give you a name. Shall I choose it?" •'

" Oh ! please," said the Ilower: • "First," said the. fairy, "you must promise to do some Work for me." .The little flower promised. " From this day you shall be called Bluebell." said the fairy,_" on condition that you ring your bell to awaken all the insects, and remind them of their duty." At night the elves tolif the humans of the new flower 'called Bluebell, and ever since the mod- est little floweret has been much loved by both fairies' and bunions.

The Nest.

Laura Promised, and the two little girls tip- toed up the stairs to the big window at the end of the hall. Every, day Laura came, over to look at the nest. with May. • Sometimes the mother was away, and then they could see the four blue- speckled eggs-:'. Their mother fed them ever so many times ,a day. and by and by they were. strong enough to flutter their wings and turn around in their nest. Then one bright morping,MaY found the nest empty. The little birgies had flown away.

The Postman. (By. Walt. Mason.)

I wonder if the postman knows How he distributes joys and woes With every trip he makes? He plods along from door to door. Makes one heart glad, another sore, And ties of friendship breaks. Ile brings a•grist of urgent duns To those distressed and sad-eyed ones Whose tailors business mean ; He trudges to the poet's home And brings him a rejected poem From haughty magazine. Oh, all there is of grief and wrath lie s;..atters as he takes his path Along the village streets; And heartaches, troubles and despair, And things that change to white the hair, Attend his toiling feet. And all there is of hope and bliss. The plighted cow, the written kiss, He carries as be wends; The letter from a roaming boy, That fills a mother's heart with joy; The greetings from old friends. The rapture of the glowing bride, The requiem of those who died, He carries in his pack; The whole long tale of human things To every village door hit brings As he pursues his track. I wonder if the postman dreams Of all the futile hopes and schemes • He carries as he walks? Of all the yearnings and the fears, nf all. the torture and the tears, The sOlaces and shocks.

CHEAPEST PLACE IN THE WORLD.

"The cheapest place in the world is Antioch, in Syria," says -a returned traveller. "Being on the Mediterranean, the climate is -just right in the colder months. I once passed a winter there, and all it cost me was a pound a week, though I leased a fine house and kept three ser- vants. For the house I paid twenty shillings a month rent, while The servants were satis- fied with two shillings a ,week. Mutton cost three-halfpence a pound. Eggs were -a penny a dozen, and ,chickens twopence-halfpenny each. The finest of fresh fruit and vegetables (in February, too!)- -were so ,cheap that they were not •sold.. in /quantity.. You got all you wanted for so much per week. All I required for my household cost 'me one shilling weeW. An American resident of Antioch told me that he and his family lived comfortably on £35 year.

TEN NIILLIONTII PASSENGER.

Not )every passenger crossing .the Atlantic has the pleasant surprise which was given a Hun- garian :emigrant named Vassily a short 'while ago. When he went to Bremen to get his ticket for the voyage to New York by the -Mrd- Veutscher-Lloyd liner George Washington, a congratulatory and smiling official of the corn-, pany told him that he Would be permitted to travel free, and, not:only so, but that he would be given a suptTior berth in the third, class instead of steeirage. The astonished Vassily thereupon inquired why he was to be so favoured, and learned, says the " flailWO and Travel Monthly,- that it was all on account of certain figures on his tieligt. It bore the num- ber 10,000,000, and therefore he was the ten !Minima' passenger to be carried by the com- pany across the Atlantic. In celebration of this .unique event, the company was granting him the special privilege of a free passage, to the land of the free.

'ME KING'S TIIEASURES

During the renovations now in progress at Buckingham Palace special precautions are to be. taken for guarding the treasure vaults on the -basernent.iloor of the _Palace. Few people even 'know of, the existence Of these vaults. They are three in number, and their contents are of almost fabulous value, comprising tho vast accumulation of jewels, armour, statutu• . pictures, furniture and other art objects which have ,come into the possession Of the Royal Family through various channels during the past two centuries, and for which no accommo- dation can be found in the public and State apartments of the Royal residences. Two of the vaults are of humense size, the largest measuring 300 ft. 'by 2601 t. These- are used for the storage of furniture, pictures,'&r: The third vault, much smaller and steel-lined throughout, is the jewel-room. The contents of .this third vault may almost be termed priceless. Of gold ornaments alone, there are over six thousand, weighing considerably over a ton in all, and many individual Hew would fetch thousands of pounds in the open market. Most of these treasures came to Queen Victoria as gifts from other Sovereigns. Every .single object in this atilt is catalogued, and the .whole collection is checked -once a year under the supeeviSion of the Keeper of the Privy Purse.

WHY AM I ILL?

"Why am I ill?" How many ask this question, and how often with a feeling of despair ! 111 again and again—day after day—headache, then- matic pains, backache, neuralgia, urinary troubles, constant tiredness, depression, loss of appetite, and apparently no cure. But it is just such common symptoms as these that should make you suspect that your kidneys are out of order. Once the trouble is traced to its cause it can be dealt with properly, and probably permanently, but only if you take a kidney medicine. Dean's Backache Kidney Pills act directly and efficaciously on the kidneys and the kidneys only. If your kidneys are weak, or diseased, and therefore unable to carry out their work, the blood becomes loaded with uric acid poison, then the whole body is slowly poisoned. Doan's Pills strengthen the Kidneys, and help them to drive these poisons out of the body. If kidney poisons are allowed to accumu- late in the system the results are such com- plaints as Dropsy, Gravel, Stone, Kidney Disorders of many kinds, Rheumatism, Lumbago, Neuralgia and Sciatica. Among the early symptoms of kidney trouble are Backache, Pains in the Loins, Headache, Urinary Troubles, " Watery Swellings round the ankles and under the eyes, Constant Tiredness and Dizziness.

THE LONG AND THREL'QUARTER COAT,- • The long coat is far too useful a possession• to be discarded, and it has an assured position during the early autumn. The ,Ahreequarter, Length is also favoured.': The new models show. greater width' on the hips, and :in some instances are fastened :like a man's overcoat, whilst, others are on the bias. • Plaids always. make their appearance' with the suintner..hOli- days ; indeed, their advent may .be ,taken as a sign that the beautiful season,. as the French Call it, is on the Wane. Many'autunnr,coats'or wraps are lined with 'plaid silk, which is also used for the revers and,cuffs. Three. large but- tons suffice on some models, but they are very large. Mantles that suggest the dolman of long, ago will be seen in brocaded Materials and vel- vets. For summer wear these charmingly quaint garments have been made in brocaded tulle on which between the patterns were bands of ribbon velvet. Other filmy materials are also used, but tulle is perhaps the most Suc- cessful, as it is so light. Tilt basque or skirt is slightly full and gathered, and the 'sleeves, which reach the elbow, • are very wide. In bright colours, these mantles, which cover the hips, to which they give the desired appear- ance of fulness, are very effective when worn with a white frock.

THE STOMACHER AND BERTHE. , Very beautiful sets of seed pearls were to be found in the jewel cases of our grandmothers, but these charming ornaments went out of fashion some time ago. The seed pearl has however, returned to favour of late, and the fortunate possessors of these old-faghioned sets are wearing the brooches and earrings, which are, in truth, pearl ornaments, for the'setting of gold is hidden by the jewels. The bathe of the period of these sets is returning, as is also the stomacher, both oi which' demand jewelled ornaments. It is quite possine to make hand- some corsage adornments of this kind with the aid of net or lace, and a goodly quantity of imitation precious and semi-precious stones, such as rhine and moonstones, turquoises and crystals, which may be bought very cheaply nowadays. Jet and other beads can also be utilised for the decoration of the evening cor- sage—where pearls are not available. TAKE CARE. OF CLOTHES. Many women, with very little comparatively to spend on dress, manage always to look neat and fresh in gowns they have Worn for quite a long time, and the reason for their spic and span appearance, that others wiih double the money to spend never attain, is to be sought in the core they bestow on their wardrobe. A good tailored suit should not be worn in the house, but be thoroughly, brushed as soon as taken off, and hung on proper hangees in a wardrobe. The brushes used for this purpose require more attention' than they usually re- ceivP. They should not be left where they can gather dust, but be kept in a box and be thor- oughly and frequently cleansed. flats also, re- quire to be brushed before being put into boxes. Dresses that are put away for the summer or winter should be cleaned if necessary, or thor- oughly brushed, pressed with a cool iron, and then carefully folded and packed away, with tissue paper in the sleeves. Tailored suits re- quire frequent pressing', and if lingeide and lace blouses are washed at home in soapy water and rinsed half a dozen times in clean water, not only will they be beautifully white, but heir length of life will be trebled.

WOMEN'S QUESTIONS. A Land in which Women Hold Equal

Position with Men. ROYALTY IN SCOTLAND.

There has been almost an unprecedented influx of Royal personages to Scotland this year. In addition to their Majesties and the Royal children, Queen Alexandra, With Princess Victoria and the Dowager Empress Marie of Russia, have been staying at Balmoral. Princess Henry of Battenberg and her sons are at Abergeldie, and the Princess Royal 'and her daughters at Mar Lodge. Prince and Prin- cess Eitel Friedrich, and Prince Joachim of Prussia have also been touring Scotland this summer, and were delighted with Scottish scenery and the beautiful city of Edinburgh. Princess Mary, who is remarkably clever, has been turning her skill in camp cooking to prac- tical account, for the benefit of some of the Royal shooting parties. As she . approaches womanhood, she shows a marked resemblance to her . grandmother, Queen Alexandra, and those who know her predict thal, she will make a brilliant and delightful woman. She is a fearless rider, a thorough sportswoman, and has retained the pretty manners for which she was distinguished as a child. A PARADISE FOR WOMEN.

Burmah would appear to be a paradise for women, although that country has China on one frontier and India on the other;--in neither. of which women hold an equal position with men. In Burniah, however, the status of the woman is equal with the man in marriage; all property belonging to a: woman before mar- riage remains hers absolutely when she be- comes a wife, and the huSband can neither sell nor alienate the joint property of himself and wife, without the latter's consent. Also divorce can be obtained Without scandal. Desertion for three years on the part of the husband, or one year on that of the wife, constitutes a suffi- cient reason in; the eye of Burmese law. A drunken, gambling, or immoral husband may be given his conge by his wife. Marriages are, as a rule, happy, and children well cared for; perhaps because it is generally recognised that

-a woi-nan confers an -honour on the man she marries, whilst in the West it is usually thought to be the other way about.

THE INCREASING. WAIST. There is no manner of doubt that the silhouette,, with its straight slender line, is threatened with extinction. The pleated basque that slightly enlarged the hips, was tenaciously introduced, and as it found favour, has been followed by the wired tunic, which altogether destroys the slim effects. This fashion cannot be termed artistic, but it will probably have a vogue, because it is a change from the straight, soft folds, which, however, are far more grace- ful and becoming. In accordion-pleated tulle, .or lace, these tunics are at their best. A great change has come over the fashionable feminine figure during the last decade; The size of the waist increases rattier than diminishes, and there is no chance Of the, re-appearance of the stylishlike figures of early Victorian days, for a generation or two, and in this respect fashion is for once highly to be commended fOr reason- ableness and good sense. STENCILLED LINEN BANDS.

Painted linen that suggests Bulgarian em- broidery is the newest kind Of trimming for simple dresses, .of the same material. Bands of foulard silk have been used for these frocks, but stencil painting on trimmings of 'a shade that contrasts with the dress is greatly to be preferred. Apple green bands oil sulphur is a striking but effective contrast, especially when the spots are stencilled in deep orange and violet tones. Sometimes a deep yoke, forming the upper part of the sleeve, is a part of the Russian tunic coat, that is so popular this sum- mer for all kinds of holiday. dresses. Two bias bands of painted linen finish the coatee just above the knees. Another novelty in conned-

ill be best." Philip rose. He thought she was merely talking about the fishermen to draw him on. "I should not speak to any of the fishermen if I were you, Miss Gould, he said. "If you will leave it to me I will do the best I can to , recover the bracelet. You had best say nothing about it." Esme looked up at him with a puzzled ex- pression. His tone was ,so cold—why, she could not imagine. Perhaps, she thought, she had somehow managed to offend him without knowing it. If so, he must soon see that she had not meant to hurt him. "Oh! that would be kind," she answered, simply. "You could arrange about it sc much better than I, especially as I am leaving so early to-morrow. If I may leave it in your hands I should be so glad." Philip bowed but did not answer. Esme felt dismayed. What could she have done to make him change like this? "Any way, I will let you know what hap- pro," said Philip, after a pause. "Will you give me your address?" Esme rose and prepared to walk home- wards, partly to hide the trembling of her lips. Something very serious must have occurred to make Philip speak as he did. She wished she could summon up courage to ask him what she had done- but after all, his manner was quite polite, and she had no right to demand anything but courtesy from him. She felt herself bound to silence, and in that silence she was conscious of all her happiness slipping far away from her, never to return. She stood for a moment looking round her. Overhead the sky was sapphire, shading to purple and silver in the east, and a faint green glow in the west. The turf below her feet was brown, the sea merely soft, neutral shadow, save where the moonlight caught the ripples. The tiny breeze had freshened, and rustled in the grass, mingling eerily with the murmur of waves far below. It was an ideal night—a night to touch and thrill the coldest heart. Yet Esnie felt no glow, only a great, overwhelming fear—fear for her life and its deadly monotony—fear for the happiness which was dying slowly, achingly, eithin her. In those few minutes the girl said good-bye to

her short dream of joy. "My address is Moor's End, Emberglade,"

ehe said. Philip jotted the three words down on his cuff, and in silence they began the descent. To Esme it was like walking home from a funeral—almost Worse. She had left behind _what was -very dear to her, and there was no hope of a sure and certain resurrection. It was gone for evermore. To the change in Philip she had. no clue; but he, attributed her silence to disappointment at the failure of her plans. He steeled himself against the appeal in her white face, the sadness in her epee, "which she could not hide. It was part and parcel of her behaviour on the cliffs. In the early dawn, when the tide was down, he would fetch her bracelet himself. She should not know that he had done so. He would not give her so much gratification. He would re- turn it without a word. He cared little about the dangers of the place. In his present reck- less mood he felt that death—even death among those cruel rocks—would be kinder than life. At the door of the hotel they parted. Philip could not meet her eyes at the last. "We shall see you in the morning?" asked Esme. "Or will you say good-bye to my aunt to-night?" "Oh—the morning, I think." answered Philip, casually wondering within himself whether the morning would find hitt, lying dead among the rocks below the Devil's Ridfze. Esme went straight up to• her room. She wanted to think, to try and divine some cause for Philip's strange and sudden coldness. Again and again she went over their conver- sation; but there was nothing in it that she could see at which he could possibly take offence. She tried to remember when it was she first noticed a difference in his manner, and thought it was about the time she dropped her bracelet. Surely he could not be offended at that! It was a careless thing to do, but nothing more. The whole affair was a mystery. She felt driven to suppose that. after all, he had only been amusing himself with her. She was far below him in social station, and men of his position were only too much inclined to play with girls in hers. :The -thought was all the more bitter because she ' truly loved him. and had believed him in- capable, of such behaviour. At last she roused heraelf and went down- stairs. It was not fair to her aunt to stay sway all the evening. She felt -weary and heavy-hearted. and she was net surprised that Mrs. Waynefieet should remark on her appearance. "How pale you look, child!" she said. "I hope you have not overtired yourself. Where did you go?" "I am a little tired," admitted Estne. "We climbed the cliffs, and went along till we were just over the Devil's Ridge." There was a little laugh as she said the words. "How romantic!" exclaimed Madame de Laze, a fat, fair woman with a cynical ex- pression. "I And did Mr. Stapylton threaten to climb the Ridgo unless you were kind to him? " "Climb the Ridge!" exclaimed Esme. "No—why should he? What do you ?swan?" "Bless the child!" cried Madame, throw- ing up her hands. "Is it possible you don't knew about the Ridge? Why, I thought every girl knew that. Only, the other day some silly fellow had a narrow escape of his life fetching some of the blue pimperneld that grow there for his lady-love." "Blue -pimpernels," repeated Esme, be "Do be more explicit, please, for I never heard of such a thing." "No, my dear, if you don't know the story of the Ridge probably you never have. They are a sort of vegetable monstrosity--sorne- thing like a -pimpernel—that grow there and nowhere else. And `because of them, or per- haps because of the extreme danger of the place, it used to be the fashion to use it as a sort of test of the affections. If a girl doubted her lover, she just dropped her glove over the cliff to see if he'd fetch it; if a girl was unkind, her lover would climb the place, and, if he survived, send her some of the blue pimpernels as a token that he 'was willing to risk his life to give her pleasure. How's that for romance?" Esme forced a laugh and a suitable re- joinder, but the room swam before her eyes. She saw the whole thing now. Philip must think her a horrid fast girl, trying to brihg him to the point of a proposal—a' silly, vain creature, anxious to boast of his danger on her behalf. No wonder his manner had altered. She would have been the same lied she been in his place. She did not know what to do' for the idea that she was so foolish once firmly rooted in his mind it would be almost impossible to make him really believe that she was innocent of any intention in dropping her bracelet. Mrs. Waynefleet's 170103 broke sharply across her thoughts. " Esme, my dear, I wish you'd go to bed. 'You look dead tired, and I don't want you to collapse to-morrow." Esme very thankfully withdrew, but she made no attempt to go to bed. Fresh horror had come to her with the thought that per- haps Philip would go himself to search for the missing treasure. He had not isaid he would employ a fisherman. Emile herself had planned that. It was quite possible that in a fit of foolhardiness he would make up his mind to do so. At all hazards it must be prevented. But how—how? She could not go to his rooms at that hour of night; she could not very well send a note without making both herself and him conspicuous; and to-morrow it might be too late. She consulted the local calendar. The tide would be law at. five o'clock in the morning. It was probable that Philip, if he meant to do so at all, would make the attempt then, to avoid the publicity of the day. It would be light by four, and she would go out and watch. At any rate she would be able to pre- vent him from beginning the climb. Of any revival of the dream between them she had no hope. She extinguished her light and drew up the blind. She would not go to bed at all, in case she fell asleep when she most wanted to be awake. And so she kept vigil, watching with tired eyes 'while the moon rose higher and 'higher and the silver pathway over the water widened. It seemed centuries before the moonlight paled in the soft flush of green herald-brightness from the east. As soon as it was ligin t3iitalgil she stole trom her room. Out in the fresh air she breathed more freely. It was but twilight yet. Philip would surely cot have started. It was comforting to be

BY DOROTHY BAIRD Author of "By the Path of the Storm," "The Turn of the Wheel," " Audrey's Old Maa." "Sister Venonica's Secret," atc.

It was a still, opal-tinted evening. To the west a dull red smouldened over the wheat- fields, but in the east all was pearly grey, for the moon was not yet risen, and sea and sky were blended together by a soft purplish haze. Even the red roofs of the town lying far below were dim and mysterious, and the lights on pier and esplanade glimmered like early-risen stars. Faint echoes of the band came up on the breeze like fairy music from far away, otherwise it was very 'still. Esnia Gould •and Philip Stapylton slowly climbed the steep path that led from the town to the top of the cliffs, talking desultory talk about trivialities and yet finding delight in so doing, as people will who know that the most important thing in the world lies• behind the nothings. Once, at a steep place, Philip had given Eame his hand to help her, and the warm grasp had thrilled them both, and they had smiled gladly at one another in the joy of youth and love. Yet only five weeks ago Esme's life had seemed to be hedged in with dreariness and monotony. Buried alive in a tiny country village miles front anywhere, too busy keep- ing house for father, brothers, and young sister to take what relaxation she might. Esme had felt she must grow old before her time, and that she was doomed to piss the whole of the rest of her life in a dreary round of uninteresting tasks. For She hated the farm and all it meant; she hated housework, with its ceaseless routine of monotony; she hated the mending which' filled her basket with a never-lessening pile. It seemed as if no time were left her in which to enjoy her youth or the sweetness of the country. _ And then had come a short emancipation. Aunt 'Hilary Waynefieet had written that her companion was taken sud- denly ill, and Eeme must come to her for a few weeks. There could be no refusing Aunt Hilary. She was the one rich relative on whom the family could rely in times of stress. She loved to ,help, but she could brook no opposition to her schemes. So Esme hur- riedly made her preparations and packed her trunk, plying Janet with instructions mean- while. Janet was nearly eighteen. It was quite time she lifted some of the burden off Esme's shoulders, but it was not without some qualms that Esme promised to obey her parting injunctions and not worry" while she was away. But once in town, the farm, the village, au Esnie's youth reasserted itself, and she tnheees.drudgery of her life faded into nothing_ drank her fill of enjoyment. The whole five weeks was just a dream of delight to her, from the Arst day, when Mrs. Waynefieet had taken her from shop to shop, fitting her with what seemed to the country girl a trousseau fit for a Duchess, down to this last evening of their stay by the sea, when she and Philip Stapylton watched the moon rise from the top of the cliffs. It had been her first glimpse into a life of ease and luxury, but, whatever fears Mrs. Waynefleet might have entertained as to the behaviour of her little niece from the country they had proved groundless. Egme wa.s not only a very pretty girl, but she was a lady to the finger-tips; and while she brought c ountry freshness into the hotel drawing -room she suggested nothing of the gatichene' of the dairy or poultry-yard, and hid what ignorances may have been hers under a charming quietness of manner. , Therefore Mrs. Wa,ynefleet had felt no pangs in introducing her niece to those of her circle who had found their way to the fashiOnable little watering-place; indeed, she had been gratified again and again by the compliments that had been paid her on Miss Gould's sweet girlish beauty and unaffected manners, and still more gratOfied when Philip Stapylton,• passing through the place on a projected motor tour along the East Coast, had •remained day after day, apparently , chained by, Esme's grey eyes and bright ways. To Esme herself the thing seemed little [short of a miracle. She was, In her own eyes, so plain and countrified that Philip's devo- tion had seemed too good to be true. He was young and handsome beyond the ordinary, and, moreover, rich enough to marry whom he would. And so when he asked her on this last evening to walk with him on the cliffs she went willingly. And now at the edge of the cliff they sat in silence- It was as if the peace and quietude of • the evening had enveloped them. One by one the stars came out, and a faint golden mist on the horizon showed where the moon would rise. "It is like fairyland," said Esme, at last. "If a knight errant on a charger came by between the wheatfields I should not be sur- prised." Philip laughed uneasily. Othel women bad talked to 'him of romance and chivalry, women whom he could not respect, and he did not want Eszne to resemble them in any way. "I am afraid I am too prosaic to believe either in fairlyland or the myths of chivalry," he said. Esme sysls disappointed. She was so con- scious 9f the bond between them that romantically and unreasonably she had al- most expected him to think as she did, or, at any rate, to sympathise with her fancies, and his answer disconcerted her. She felt ner- vous, partly because she knew, as she could not fail to know, what was in his heart. She commenced toying with a bracelet that she always wore —:- almost her only piece of jewellery—swinging it backwards and for- wards in her fingers; just to occupy herself, and bridge over a silence that seemed full of embarra-ssment. Suddenly, without any Warning, the brace- let slipped from her fingers and shot over the edge of the cliff. It hung there poised for a moment, gleaming in the soft light, and then' fell. Esme watched it with dismay in her eyes, then she turned to Philip. "Oh, what shall I do?" she cried, in great distress. "It was my mother's." Philip did not answer at once. The inci- dent was unfortunate, coming as it did after the little conversation about chivalry, and also because the spot where they were sitting was full of romantic associations. Just below them, about midway down the cliff, was the Devil's Ridge—a ledge of rock, quite ina.cces- sible from above, but capable of attainment from below. The climb was so dangerous that from time immemorial it had been used as a test of love. If a maiden doubted her lover's affection, she would send him to fetch some trinket she had dropped over the Ridge, as Fame had dropped her bracelet. If, on the other hand, the lady proved unkind, her lover would fetch her 'some of the strange blue flowers which grew there and there only. It seemed impossible that Esme should not know of the Devil's Ridge and its story. Philip had thought her so different from other girls, and yet she was showing herself as vain and foolish as the rest. A feeling of bitter disappointment filled him, but he fought it down, trying to believe she was ignorant of the legend. His next words were more or less of a question. He rose and looked over the edge. - "It has, of course, fallen on the Devil's Ridge," be said. Now Esrae had heard that there was such a place, but she had noticed that people gene- ally laughed when they spoke of climbing the Ridge, and she supposed there was some joke about it. iOf danger n Connection with it, or romance, she never dreamt, and, therefore, she fell into the trap. "The Devil's Ridge!" she exclaimed. "Then there is a chance of getting back my bracelet. Oh! lam glad." , Philip was silent again. He was beginning to think that his goddess was only a goddess of clay, full of self-importance and vanity. After all, he thought, perhaps it was just as well tha,t she had shown herself in her true colours before he had pledged himself to her. A girl who could calmly send a man who loved her into such danger, just to gratify what must be foolish pride and silly love of romance, could not be worth haslng. Yet the ache of disappointment was hard to bear, and his tone was very bitter when he replied. , " Oh! of course it can be found," he said. "It will rr,,an waiting till the tide soca down. that is all." He thought it superfluous to 80. Q that it also meant risking .a human life. • "IlLe.a." said Zsx.:s, innoccatly, "I must

HOW "BUFFALO BILL" GOT HIS NAME,

Coronel William F. Cody, the "Buffalo Bill" Of "Wild West Show" fame, has just retired and sold up his show, having toured the world since 1883. He was born in Scott County, lowa, in 1846. For severkil years he was fam- ouS as a United States Government scout and

guide, and is said to have taken part in more Indian battles than any other living man. At the battle of Indian Creek he killed Yellow Hand, the Cheyenne chief, in a hand-to-hand tight. • About the year 1867 he contracted to furnish the Kansas Pacific Railway with all the

_ buffalo meat required to feed the labourers en- gaged on the work of construction, and in ful- filment of the contract killed no fewer than 4,280 buffaloes in 18 months, thus earning the name of "Buffalo Bill." •

Asa young man Cody followed the ocaupa. ,ion of express rider for many months, and, 11

was during this period that he made his ride of 320 miles in 21 hours 40 •minutes. On one occasion, while carrying the mails, he_ saw .a revolver pointed at him as he flew around a bend in. a narrow pass, and heard a highway- man shout, " Halt ! Throw up your hands." He did so, and the footpad then said, I don't

want to hurt you, boy, but I ,do want them bags." As the outlaw reached for his booty, however, Cody made the pony plunge; with the result that the robber was thrown and Atunned Disdounting, the lad disarmed and then pin- ioned his adversary, and when the bandit came to his senses made him remount -and ride as a prisoner to the next station.

At Kingston, when a woman Was summoned for using abusive language to another „woman, the defendant said: "Look here. your worships, if we had our proper rights, both of us. we oufeit to have, fourteen days apiece. I know it would quieten her and me too."

Preparations for the Coming Season

at Wilderspool.

WARRINGTON MEN GETTING INTO FORM.

Notes by "Scrum."

"King Football" is getting out his robes (players usually call them "togs ") and polish- ing up his crown, so that he may be ready for

his eight months of reign. A couple of days hence the winter pastime .will have started ill some places, but Warrington is not. displaying as much haste as some teams to maul the ball. We shall, a week to-day, have our. first -football baptism, but I do not think Warrington are acting unwisely in waiting six days tater the official kick-off of the game.

A Monday night tmatch might have been acceptable to those who are about as enthusias- tic over football as are the sportsmen who must be out after .the partridges on the " gloricu", first of September, but while theymay be dis- appointed a little by having to Wait another six days, they Must nurse their disappointment, and 'perhaps it will tend to make them even more enthusiastic followers of the club. how- ever, they can gratify their appetite a little by patronising the practice match to-day. the pro- ceeds of which are to help local charities.

SPECULATION STAGE.

We are now at the stage when we have to flourder in the mire of speculatiOn. On every hand questions arise like these: "Will War- rington do better this year?" "Are they likely to win the Cup?" "Do you think they will be champions?" and so on. If I wished to gratify every questioner, the reply would be in the affirmative, as they, say in Parliament. The best answer, however, is "Wait and see." ...Still every indication seems, to .point to a most suc- cessful season, even a better one than last, and if the team gives, by their performances, a reply of "Yes" to the queries above, every Warring- tonian will be gratified. At all events, we are optimistic enough to anticipate still greater deeds in the forthcoming season.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0195.txt | included=true | included =====
CONFIDENCE IN THE TEAM.

The committee have the greatest confidence the team they will put into the field for the

first match on 6th September. "We have got some of the finest material in the Northern Union to pick and choose from," said one offi- cial, "and with that as a start we ought to do extremely well."' The men, too, I learn, are showing unbounded eagerness in the prepara- tions for a start. There is a healthy rivalry between them in the matter of "getting fit," and they are nqt sparing of themselves in their training. Thy have much enthusiasm, and

this, combined with fitness, gives ample pro- mise of success. A speedy lot; too, they are expected to prove, and the cult of scientific tackling has been closely studied, so the team promises to be an all-round one as affects every department of the 'game. " •

===== 0002601_19130830_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
A DRESS REHEARSAL.

To-day we are promised a full-dress rehearsal, and after seeing this practice match one will be better able to discuss the merits .Of the team. Two strong sides will take .the 'turf, and the final selections will be made just .before the practice match begins, There is an abundance of material from. which the committee can choose. The final selection 'will be from amongst the following :--L B. -Jolley and J. Brown,- full-backs ; E. Brookes, J. Tranter, A. Renwick, Howard Davis, ' B. Bradshaw, G. Dickenson,' T. C. Bardsley, H. TIIE OPENING MATCH.

It is a " leetle " premature to write about the opening match, but already an auspicious start is assured. The pitch is. in good trim, and, come rain or shine in the interval, nothing can prevent it being in. a good condition for the fixture with Leigh next Saturday. By then

the committee will have carefully weighed up the capabilities of the men, and, perchance, may have discovered some dark horse likely to turn out a crack, and they will be in a position to put their best in the fielit Leigh and Warring- ton usually produce a good match when they meet, and, if rumour proves a truthful ,jade this time, Leigh is likely to give Warrington a close .run in the fixture on Satuiday. From a spectacular point of view, a well-balanced fight is desirable to a runaway affair, and a close game is just what the .Warrington men require to cap their training.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
HEAVY MONTH BEFORE THEM.

Warrington have a heavy month before them for a start, and they will have to put Me best leg. forward. The Leigh match at home will prove no sinecure, and on the Bth September, two days after the, opening game; they are due at Huddersfield. It is rather hard that War- rington should be called upon to face the York- shire cracks at so early a date, but the men are quite, enthusiastic at the prospect of such a meeting, and in N.U. Bugby, as in anything else, enthusiasm is a valuable asset. The 'Ol- - Saturday, 13th September, they net the " Batla Boys" at 'Mount Pleasant. Ball are reputed to be a strong_ side, but whi.Ther they are capable of beating Warrington remains to be seen. Broughton Bangers visit us on 2.oth September, and this match shoUlci be War- ringtOn's; as also should that played at Swinton the following Saturday. -

ANGLING.

Warrington Workingmen's Association. N.A. CENTRE.

THE ENGLISH CHAMPIONSHIP.

The thampionship match promoted by the "Daily Mirror" is to be held on the Dee, near Chester, on September 22nd. The Northern Anglers' Association have the arrangement's in hand. The Match Committee wet at Chester .the other day to go over the course. Represen- tatives from Bolton, Crewe, St. Helens, Chester, Wigan and Warrington .x.vere present. The honour of pegging out the length' fell to War- rington, and Messrs. J. Bryers, J. Warburton, H. Moores and W. Taylor were selected to peg out the length, along with Messrs. A. Stout and J. Potter, who are acting as superintendents with the committee during the match. The arrangements are: Competitors to fish the left bank of the river, the centre being the Earl Grosvenor Iron Bridge; draw about 10.30 a.m.; commence fishing at 11 a.m., until four p.m., a matter of five hours' angling; sizeable fish only to weigh, according to rule. Each competitor will be provided with rules before the match. Steamers are being chartered to take anglers and friends to the Iron Bridge. The steamers will leave Chester landing stage at 9 a.m., and return to Chester at 5.30 p.m. Warrington anglers should take advantage of seeing this contest, as it may be many years before it comes our way again, unless the pro- moters think the Dee is an ideal spot for their match and they desire a repetition. Anglers locally may rest assured that nothing will left to to chance with the Warrington workers to bring about this result. ANNUAL MATCH. The annual match, held September 14th, is to be combined with the officials' match, the offi- cials to fish one hour after the big contest. About £5O worth of prizes have been sent in by a well-known firm to select from, and this match will be a bigger affair than ever. We are expecting a record entry—the same as over the opening match. Members should book their names at the club houses for the convey- ances, as it makes the secretaries,' duties lighter. All members must be fully paid up before they can compete in the match, and entries must be made on the Saturday night before the match.

THE CENTRE NIATCH. The competitors who succeeded in winning their way to this match will fish off at New- bridge, in the same length where they won the honour. The lop weight of this match will go to the final at Carr Mill Dam, near St. Helens. Train leaves Bank Quay at 1.30 p.m. on Satur- day next for Hartford, match, to commence at three pan., until five p.m., the winner to go to the final and receive no less than is. 6d.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0205.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTON.

The canal at Norton has fished very badly this last month, and a large number of roach have been seen on top dead in the basin behind the warehouses. If anglers remember, the same thing occurred last season. We do not think it is the cause of pollution from the Acton Bridge- length, as a fair catch of roach was taken by W. Birchall in the Preston Brook length, near the crane, no later than Monday last. A.s this is about half a mile nearer the Acton Bridge length, it goes to show that fish are all right there. Still, we would be more satisfied to see good water coming through the tunnel, as the pollution from the upper reaches Must do our canal considerable harm, although we get the current from both canals at the cross-cuts at Preston Brook, and any pollution coming from the Acton Bridge length must be considerably weakened before coming down Norton length. We have had reports as to certain articles falling off the boats in the basin; others report that in their opinion it has been blown up. If this is so, let us hope the culprit will be caught and punished accordingly. Mr. J. Clare, secretary of the Runcorn Angling Club, has the matter in hand, and the angler who favours this spot may be assured that he will make every inquiry into the Matter. •

THE GOWY. . Several members visited here last week-end, but results were poor. The fresh coming down stream was almost a copper colour, and when the stream is in this condition very little is done. OTHER PLACES.

Hartford vas again visited by a fair number of locals. Outside the contest which took place very little was done amongst the specimens, with the exception of Mr. fl. Bradbury's gud- geon, loz. 12dr., which now tops the set. The best place of the week was Chester Canal. Sev- eral N.A.A. officials visited this length and saw a member with a fair catch of roach. He had 12 fish, scaling about 'Jib.; the smallest was about Goz. Next week will be published the prizes for the annual match. A. S.

• An exciting °finish was witnessed in the. final of •the Warrington and District Bowling, Asso- ciation -Championship, played on the green of the Cemetery Hotel on Saturday, for tho!' A. H. Crosfield " Cup. •

Play proved of much merit and there were some close finishes. Despite the early rain, the green played well, and the 'spectators wit- nessed an excellent afternoon's sport. The Cemetery Hotel team won the game by 51 points, but they were eight down upon the two home-and-home fixtures, and the Conservatives thus became champions. The details were :

Cemetery Hotel. Conservatives. W. Barton . 21 H P Turner rt A. Ford 2l H. Hughes (capt.)... 20 T. Harrison ...... W. J. Woods (ca pt.). 21 W. Millington R. Antrobus .... ....... 14 T. Horrabin ....... C. Smith T. N. Fielding G. Ball, jun. A. Forster 2l W Thomasson lO T. Quarrie F. Yarwood J. Ashton 2O A Scott

At, the -close the cup wa,s ,presented by the Mayor to the champions. I understand the cup, which I have to pre- sent to the Conservatives as champions," said the Mayor, "is the gift of Mr. A. H. Crosfield, and therefore it seems to me somewhat re- markable, perhaps not from a political point of view, but merely as a coincident, that the cup presented to the association by Mr. A. H. Cros- field should go to the party opposite in politics to that which he belongs." (Laughter.) Real merit, he added, had won the cup for the 'Conservatives, and while he congratulated them, he also congratulated' the losers upon the splendid fight they had made. He was also pleased to see the friendly spirit which existed between the members of both team.

"1 have not been to Goodwood," he said, "but I have seen some very good wood races this afternoon.'" (Laughter.) Mr. H. Hughes, the Conservative skipper, in accepting the cup, said it was a credit to the Conservatives to have won the billiard cham- pionship and the bowling championship in the same year.

BOWLING.

Contrary to general expectations, Rylands' did not win the " Crosfield " Cup in the final played on the Red Lion Hotel green at Prestwich on Saturday. They did not play their accustomed game, and were defeated by a majority of 56.

. The heavy rain of the morning made the green sticky, anti this seemed to affect the Rylands' bowlers more than the members of the Wigan Subscription. The result was that in many instances the bowls were played short, or too strongly, and consequently _overran the jack.

Wigan adiusted their play more easily to the prevailing conditions, and this gave them an advantage in a game in. which there was little to pick and choose between the aides in point of skill.

The Warringtonians started well, and the first two on the list succeeded in pulling off victories. J. Langley, with excellent play, defeated F. Bullen, the Wigan Subscription captain, 21-18; and J. Gleave beat T. Ball, 21-19.

The only other Warrington -man to make full points was H. Langley, who won with 21-18. For Bylands', S. Massey fought gallantly, but was defeated by his opponent by the odd point, 21-20. A similar fate, after skilled play, befel T. Humphries. The other Rylands' members suffered .rather heavily.

Wigan won by 244 to 188, and in mesenting the prizes Mr. Coombs, president of the British Crown Green Association, paid a tribute to the skill shown by both teams. Although defeated, he said the Rylands' men were not disgraced.

A splendid array of bowlers turned up in the Liberal Association hapdicap on Saturday after- noon, and some excellent bowling was witnessed by a big crowd. Over 130 games were played, and it is very evident the promoters of this handicap were highly pleased with themselves.

It has certainly come to stay. Mr. Campbell, the proprietor, has spent both money and time to keep the green in excellent order, and it is doubtful if any green in Warrington looked better than the Bank Quay green did on Satur- day, especially considering the number of enthu- siasts who use the green during the evenings.

There should be a big crowd to-day to witness the completion of the handicap, some of the players left in being in the Brat flight. The ten minutes' rule will be strictly adhered to to-day.

Crosfield's No. 1 team secured a handsome victory over the Penketh Tannery ,in the War- rington and District Consolation Final, played at Rood-lane, winning by a majority of 42. Crosfield's maintained a hold on the games all through, and only two Tannery men were able •to run up to the full points.

• An interesting game was that between liessrs. Jenks and Gregson. At the fourteenth end they peeled, 10-10, and two ends later it was 12-12. Then the Tannery man shot ahead and kept a lead until the 29th end, when the couple peeled, 19-19. After that Mr. Jenks had it his own way.

A very bad start marked the play of Mr. Ellison (Crosficld's), who was matched against Mr. D. Finch. The Penketh bowler got a lead at the start, and at the 11th end was nine ahead. Then fortune came Ellison's- way, and at the 21st end peeled, 16-16. A final two, however, gave victory to the Penketh player.

As was anticipated, the Cemetery Hotel team were too good all round for the Junior Conser- vatives, whom they defeated on the Cemetery green by a majority of 51 points, but in the pre- vious match the Conservatives were 58 to the good, and thus won the championship by seven points. The scores on Saturday were: Ceme- tery, 225; Conservatives, 174.

The "White Heather Day" match at the Horse and Jokey, under the auspices of the Warrington Works Association and the Cale- donian Association, played in aid of local char- ities, resulted in a close game, the Works win- ning with the margin of nine points.

LADIhS' BuwLING MAIN. Keen and Exciting Games.

It was the ladies' day at the King and Queen Hotel on A hursday, when the President and Mrs. Steel invited the wives and lady friends of the members of the bowling club to meet them for a game of bowls. 'lite weather was ideal, and tue green in perfect condition, the beautilul garden surrounding the green en- hancing the pleasure of ihe afternoon's' enjoy - merit. The ladies turned Out in large numbers, and many of them showed great proficiency in the art of trundling, and several exciting games were' witnessed during the course of the after- noon. The members of the commitree of the bowling club were present to instruct the ladies on questions of bias, and to referee the games. Mr. Steel acted as referee for several of the games, and Mrs. Steel was amongst the com- petitors, although she was thrown out in the first round by a lady who reached the final. About 4.30 a break was made in the games, when the guests, on the invitation of Mrs. Steel, joined with her in afternoon, tea, which was taken on the precincts of the .green, and was greatly enjoyed, the gentlemen acting as waiters. Every preparation had been made for the enjoyment of the ladies, and Mr. and Mrs. Starkey are to be congratulated on the admir- able way in which the arrangements had been carried out. Prizes had been provided for every oompetitor. Several/ photographs were taken, and everyone went ;away' greatly pleased with the afternoon's enjoyment. Appended is a list of the scores:—

BOWLS FOR CHARITY.

Mayor Participates in a Keen Game.

WORKS ASSOCIATION WIN.

The members cif the Works' Association and the Caledonian " Association were engaged bowling for charity on the Horse and Jockey green on Saturday afternoon, and while the match proved profitable for charity, it proved most pleasant for the players. The Works Association won by nine points. The MAyoa, who Was one of the Caledonian team, prior to the start of the match com- mended thd object for which it was played. He was sure, he said, he had only to mention the Nursing Association and the Children's Sum- mer Camp to enlist the sympathy of everyone at such a Warrington gathering. ",l am taking part in the game," said the Mayor, "and I hope I won't disgrace myself by playing a bad game. I must plead guilty to being a mere novice, but I will do my best, and hope you will be very indulgent with me." (Laughter and applause.) The first game was played between the Mayor and Mr. J. Fulton-Smith, who were pitted against Councillors J. Wilkinson and T. Locker. After a keen game the• mayoral side was defeated 15-13. . Some other close encounters took place, as the following details show : -

Works Association. Caledonian Association. Coun. J. Wilkinson The Mayor Coun.- T.. Looker 15 Mr. J. Fulton-Smith 13 Coun. J. Halliwell Mr. F. Plumpton Mr. J. Leigh l3 Mr. S. Grant l5 Mr. J. Stanley Mr. S. Shave l5 Mr. J. Aitken ........ 7 Mr. T. Bunch .....

Mr. T. Cooper' 12 Mr. P Lyle l5 Mr. T. Reddish Mr, R. Miteheil Mr. T. Bennett ...... 14 Mr. J. Bell Mr. A. Lingard Mr. J. Wilkinson ... Mr. G. Ward Mr. R. Ilarrilcn .... Mr. J. Stewart

Mr. J. llibbtt l5 Mr. D. R. Hodge .... 14 Mr. J. Ashton Mr. F. Dale 6 Mr; J T. Smith l5 Mr. C. Trowe Mr. H Bell r. A. E. Burdett... 15 Mr. J. Callender, jun. 2 Mr. J. Willett ........ Ald. W. J. Forshaw Mr. A. Peekett 7 Mr. W Murray 15 Mr. J. Broadhurst.. MT. P. H. Brown Coun. Jenkins ........ 15 Mr. J. Robinson ll

142 133 The Works Association won by nine points.

AFTER MATCH SPEECHES.

Mr: W. PATRICK announced the result of the match and referred to the keen interest both sides displayed in the game. Mr. J. WiLizrr (chairman of the Works Bowl- ing Association) expressed the pleasure which the members of his association found in the annual match with the sturdy Caledonians. He said he was glad, whiter enjoying themselves on the green, that bowlers were not forgetful of the lot of less fortunate brethren. Through making that match a success, they were enab- ling poor children to spend healthful days at the seaside. They were also aiding the work of the Nursing Association, the value of which was realised by hundreds of working men. (Applause.) The Mayor having gone to fulfil anQther bowling engagement, Alderman FORSHAW (deputy-mayor) replied for the Caledonians. Although they had not secured victory, they had given a good account of themselves. He pro- mised that next year they would certainly win. (Laughter.) It was said a Scotsman was never more•at home than when he was abroad, and, wherever he happened to go, he was usually .successful. He had therefore confidence that victory would fall to the Caledonians on . the next occasion. He hoped in the meanwhile tile Works Association would continue to pros- per in the good work it was accomplishing in the interests of the game of bowls. (Applause.) Mr. W. MURRAY paid a tribute to the game, and said he felt that the man who, at the close of a day's work. elected to spend his time on the green would never go far wrong. (Ap- plause.) Councillor JENKINS thanked the two bowling sides for the practical interest displayed by them in the Children's Summer Camp. Every year the work on behalf of the poor children was being extended. This season nearly 600 boys and girls had been sent to camp at Pres- tatyn. At the present moment' poor and weakly children, who had been recptnmended to the committee by ministers and friends, were enjoying the benefits of freedom and fresh air at the camp. (Applause.) On behalf of the District Nursing Association, Mr. H. HOPE expressed thanks. He hoped the Warrington bowlers would long continue to combine recreation with the desire to benefit institutions so deserving. (Applause.) Afterwards the players sat down to Ica, and later an impromptu concert was presided over by Mr. W. Murray. Songs wee given by the following: Messrs. Burns. J. Aitken. J. Robert- son, C. Trowe, J. Willett, G. Marshall, \V. Murray, n. Ferguson, and Mr. Chat tenon. Mr. F. Plumnton rendered a stirring recitation, entitled "War, what is it?" .

Votes of thanks were accorded to the joint secretaries, Mr. J. Tennant nnd Mr. W. Patrick. Also to Mr. and Mrs. Rughworth for the excellent way they had catered for the com- pany, both on and off the green.

RINK HOikri:-

WARRINGTON v. WIGAN. A rink hockey • match was played on Satur- day evening at the Skating Rink, when the Warrington Empire Hall drew with Wigan Empire, the scores being three goals each. Barres scored all the three goals' for Wigan, and the Warrington scorers were C. Davis (2) and W. Whittaker (1). The game proved a keen and exciting one.

RYLANDS' FOOTBALL TEAM

Anticipations of a Good and Successful

Season

PRACTISING WELL.

We learn from Mr. Burgess, the secretary of the Rugby section .of • the Rylands' Recreation Club, that the team is turning out well at prac- tice, in view of the final tie for the Manchester Shield, which takes place at Cadishead against Weaste, on September 13. ,The season .opens at home against Ditton next Saturday, a fact which will be relished by the members, who are anxious to get to serious business.

- All last season's players are available, which means that the team which was clever enough to win the First Division Manchester Cham- pionship ,the first year of promotion will be in the field to defend its laurels. '

'Viands' are running a second team this season, in the St. Helens and District League, if they get permission from the works council. They regret there is not a junior league in War- rington. Such a league would be sure to bene- fit the senior club, besides finding pastime on a Saturday afternoon for the juniors. It will give satisfaCtion to the members of both clubs to know tilat the Warrington Club Execu- tive have promised to -give Rylands' two Com- bination dates . this seli.'son. The days these matches are played will be really local "Derby" days.

PALACE_ AND HIPPODROME.

The variety fare, at the Hippodrome this week is of a most interesting type.,- The KO Ten Icht Troupe of . Japanese conjuilors --give a. clever illusteation of the Eastern phase of mysticism. Their tricks are neatly executed and the water trick, in which sprays of liquid squirt out of most unlikely places, js a smart and puzzling bit of conjuring. The 'Waldrons are masters of &IMO and,' prOvide the merriest of turns. Charles Einerald and Maude Dupre, in "A Hot Scotch," keep the humour. going, and they pro- vide some excellent dancing. Lynn and Leslie, "the frisky duo," justify that title, and the Other has, including the Bros. Darras, in a sensa- itillial trapeze act, are of first class merit.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0240.txt | included=true | included =====
NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

The Hippodrome Tmanagement are simply floodino us with good things. Following upon a Splendid bill this week, an excellent pro- gramme is promised next week. The Six Brothers Luck top the bill with "The Demon of the Cellar," a farcical sketch, which is one of .best laughter provokers on tour. There is no possibility of anyone being in the dumps when the Lucks are about. -Rosie Heys is a talented new vocalist. Millie Payne will present her London successes, including the popular "Waltzing." ,Baker and Gee are droll Yankee piano performers. The Two Butterflies, grace- ful and artistic gymnasts, are also on the bill with "A gymnastic poem." The other turns are all good and the programme is one of the strongest seen for some time..

THUM ROYAL.

HUNTING BIG GAME_ WITH. THE CINEMA

€LtMEfIA.

_ On Monday Messrs. Frances Clare and Bom- berger will enter upon a fortnight's presenta- tion at Scotland-road of a marvellous 'picture, "Nature's Zoo," by Cherry Kearton, "- the man who dared." There will be two houses nightly and matinees on Mondays, Thursdays and Saturdays. - There have been many mighty 7 6unters since the days of Nimrod, and trophies of the chase are exhibited to .sh.ow their prowess These. hunters have been Satisfied to 11,et within rifle shot itheir prey; to 'stalk .their game- froM. a safe distance. But it Was left to Mr. Cherry Kearton, a native of Yorkshire, to hunt big game with a cinema camera, and to show us " stay-at-homes " what it is like td rub shoulders with the wild beasts of strange countries in their native lairs.

Always an enthusiastic naturalist, Mr. Kear- ton, in early boyhood, commenced to photo- graph the birds and animals of England. The invention of the bioscope gave him an oppor- tunity of taking a series of moving pictures, Portraying the life and habits, of British. birds and fauna.

The success of this exhibit attracted wide- spread notice, and Mr. Keartdn- and his brother were summoned by the King (when Prince of Wales) to lecture before the Royal Family. Then came the tall of the wold, and Mr. Kearton went forth on as adventurous an under- taking as is given to few men to accomplish. Into the jungles of India went "the man who dared," and we can see him riding on the back of an elephant through the heart of our great and mysterious Empire. We are with .him in the primeval forest, and can almost, hear the chattering of monkeys as they leap from tree to tree in the branches overhead. Wild elephants feed so near that we hold our breath in fear of discovery. A great python glides its 16 feet of .sinuous length along a fallen tree but a few steps away. ' Penetrating the interior: of I3orneo Mr. Rear- ton contiflues his series of moving spictures, showing the life and habits of the natives and wild animals of that. savage and little-known country, The Bock Mountain bears in,Ameriett and the mouse- of Canada are "snapped " by Mr. Kean, ton as he hunts through ," Nature s Zoo," in his world-quest for big game. And then with Buffalo- Jones' American Cow- boys, he goes to British East Africa, imperilling his life in photographing the rhinoceros and the lion, the giraffe and zebra of the ," Dark Continent"

Eland and Kongoni . are pursued and, cap- tured; the cowboys are demonstrtiting the pos- sibility of lassoing big .game, am! we -see them setting forth from Nairobi amidst, the sceptical jeers of native spcirtsmen.

TERRITORIALS IN CAMP.

Interesting Brigade Sports and Pretty Torchlight Tattoo.

A CONVOY SCHEME.

Denbigh, Saturday.

To-day we turned out of our beds early, to find the rain had cleared, and there was great promise of a line day. Yesterday's rain had cleared the air a little, and, in the words of a "Terrier," ".Everything in the garden .was lovely." The .Warrington lads -took part in a very well executed convoy scheme on the hills Overlooking Denbigh. • In the afternoon the brigade sports were held, a portion of the parade ground having been marked out fbr the purpose. A special feature of the afternoon was the splendid work of the Warrington- learn in the half-mile marching competition, in full marching order.. Under: Sergt. Bottomley the men marched round the! course ." like a line regiment," as the Brigadier ; was heard to remark. The Warrington men also won the tent-pitch- i ing competition and the tug-of-war, the latter! event-proving that weight does not always count! The Scottish battalion won tha relay race aidi the 100 yards and half-mile. The number of second prizes which fell to the Scottish enabled; them, to curry. off . the 'Brigade Cup,. which had 'been presented by the Denbigh Reception Coin-A inittee for annuai competition in the above Shq events. TORCHLIGHT TATTOO. I One or two slight showers .fell during tht progress of the sports,. but on the whole, the Weather- was all: that could be desired. A tordh- light tattoo was held in the evening, and was very well attended by the residents of Denbigh and neighbourhood, who freely expressed their delight at the marching and counter-marching of the men. The night was pitch dark, and the figures the men made, were extremely well thrown up by the torches. Earlier in the day the 4th South Lancashire machine gun section (under Lieut.• L. S. Henshall) carried off the cup for the beg machine gun.section.in the brigade. ; A. forge number of the men-left camp to-day for Rhyl, Colwyn Bay pild Llandudno.

DEPARTURES AND ARRIVALS. Sunday. The usual church parade was held to-day, and the .11ev. W. Bracecamp preached to the whole brigade, except the Scottish battalion, taking the life of Joshua as an example for the men. The weather was line,, and a great crowd of visitors .arrived in camp. _ _ _ _

- During the day Serit. J. T. Houghlon and Corporal C. J. Sheash were presented with the T.F. efficiency medal. We lost Lieuts. Whittle and Skinner and 65 men, who were unable 10 stay for the full fortnight, and about 20 men arrived to tiike their places. In the evening Mr. Bracecamp preached a very eloquent sermon at the Church of St., Marcella (which, by the way, is nearly 1,400 years old), and a good number of Territorials and visitors attended. A service was also held in the Y.M.C.A. tent.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0243.txt | included=true | included =====
A SHAM FIGHT.

Monday. Breakfast was served at seven o'clock this morning, and soon afterwards "A" Company turned out with white hat-bands to take up a position. The rest or us followed in an holz, to attempt to dislodge them. Blank ammunition was carried, and the rattle of musketry was soon heard in all quarters. The "enemy "were first discovered in a gully, and on being attacked they.,were driven on to a hill, which they 'held for a time, but in the end' they had to submit to superior numbers. The battalion arrived back in can at 2.30 p.m.

, . The signalling section of the despatch r4iders were detailed to tale part in the annual inspec- lion,•and :received hikti praise' for the way they performed their duties.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0244.txt | included=true | included =====
A FINE BRIGADE.

'On the arrival in camp, .trh-e-- brigade were drawn up in - Square and addressed by, the Brigadier, Colonel Mactle. He Said he was proud to -command such a fine brigade as the South -Lancashire Brigade. He specially com- plimented the 4th South 'Lancashires. Although;- on paper; they were the Weakest battalion, the Warrington men were the stron'g- :est batthlion in Camp'. 'He appealed to all ink to -"play" the game and do their utmost to do credit to their company, their ,battalion, and the Whole brigade. . The "old soldiers" could help the recruits ih many little ways by teaching thctrn various details of camp life, and the recruits should try and feel as important a member of the brigade as the brigadier himself. The football competition was continued this evening; when the band, after a gruelling game, 'beat "1) " Company by two goals three corners to one goal one corner, while "C" Company settled the account of " F " Company to the tune of two goals . three corners to two goals one corner. - RETAINED THE CUP.

Tuesday.

The Brigade Cup, .fOr drill .and ge-n-e-r-a-re—M7 ciency, was competed for to-day. " A " Com- pany was selected to represent the battalion in the competition, and had the honour of retaining tub cup for us. Inc battalion formed up at. 1.1 o'clock and marched about four miles along the Bettwys-y-Coed road. On arrival, uncooked rations were served out, and the men made fires ad cooked their own dinner's.

In the afternoon we advanced an practised a night :attack. Tea was served ,on the bivouac ground, and the men settled clown for the night. At 2 a.m. we were quietly roused from , our slinnbers and donned our equipment in darkness. The performande of the afternoon was repeated in the dark, amid absolute' silence. The conthfct of the whole of the men during the manceuvre was deserving of great praise. We arrived back in camp at 6 a.m. On Tues- day evening ,the regimental band gave a fine performance in the town,, and were heartily cheered by the large crowd which assembled. The Mayor voiced the feelings of the audience in a neat little, speech.

===== 0002601_19130830_art0245.txt | included=true | included =====
POPULAR BRIGADIER.

Wednesday. The Warrington lads were again On the Move at 8.15, and marched five Miles, to attack the 10th Liverpools, who had taken up a pOsition on the hills near Llanrhaidr. The battalion Was to-day un4ler the connnand of Major Lloyd, 9th .Liverpools, who. is qualifying for promo- tion. At one of the halts on the-roadside, the Brigadier, Colonel Wale, spoke to a group of our men very encouragingly. "Our" Briga- dier (as the whole of the brigade call him) in- quired as to how We -enjoyed- the bivouac the P''°" s night, and congratulated us on our conduct there.

Shme Colonel 11/lacfle's appointment., two years ago, he has found his way to the hearts of all ranks by his homely, encouraging manner. fie -is always ready with a cheery word to life up fallen hearts; and cheer the men on their way.
